### Extreme Value Modeling for Detection and Attribution of Climate Extremes

Extreme Value Modeling for Detection and Attribution of Climate Extremes Jun Yan, Yujing Jiang Joint work with Zhuo Wang, Xuebin Zhang Department of Statistics<#sep#>Implement a stack using only instances of a queue. Your stack should support the following operations: `push(x)`, `pop()`, `top()`, and `empty()`. The queue class has methods `enqueue(x)`, `dequeue()`, `peek()`, and `is_empty()`.

**Input:**
- A series of operations to be performed on the stack. Each operation is one of the following:
  - `push x`: Push element `x` onto the stack.
  - `pop`: Remove the element on top of the stack.
  - `top`: Get the top element of the stack.
  - `empty`: Return whether the stack is empty.

**Output:**
- For each `top` operation, print the top element of the stack.
- For each `empty` operation, print `True` if the stack is empty, `False` otherwise.

**Sample Input:**
```
push 1
push 2
top
pop
empty
```

**Sample Output:**
```
2
False
```

```python
class Queue:
    def __init__(self):
        self.items = []
    
    def enqueue(self, x):
        """Add an element to the end of the queue."""
        self.items.append(x)
    
    def dequeue(self):
        """Remove and return the element from the front of the queue."""
        if self.is_empty():
            raise IndexError("dequeue from empty queue")
        return self.items.pop(0)
    
    def peek(self):
        """Return the element at the front of the queue without removing it."""
        if self.is_empty():
            raise IndexError("peek from empty queue")
        return self.items[0]
    
    def is_empty(self):
        """Check if the queue is empty."""
        return len(self.items) == 0

class StackUsingQueue:
    def __init__(self):
        self.queue = Queue()

    def push(self, x):
        """Push element x onto the stack."""
        self.queue.enqueue(x)
        q_size = len(self.queue.items)
        for _ in range(q_size - 1):
            self.queue.enqueue(self.queue.dequeue())

    def pop(self):
        """Remove and return the element on top of the stack."""
        if self.queue.is_empty():
            raise IndexError("pop from empty stack")
        return self.queue.dequeue()

    def top(self):
        """Get the top element of the stack."""
        if self.queue.is_empty():
            raise IndexError("top from empty stack")
        return self.queue.peek()

    def empty(self):
        """Check if the stack is empty."""
        return self.queue.is_empty()

# Example usage:
# my_stack = StackUsingQueue()
# my_stack.push(1)
# my_stack.push(2)
# print(my_stack.top())	# Should print 2
# print(my_stack.pop())	# Should print 2
# print(my_stack.empty())  # Should print False

Wait, but earlier we used injectivity, but how did we get injectivity? We had f(a)=f(b) implies a=b, which we proved. So injective. However, to have an inverse function, we need bijectivity (injective and surjective). But since f is injective and maps R+ to R+, unless it's also surjective, the inverse may not exist on the entire R+. But in our substitution above where we set y = f^{-1}(t), we need that for any t in R+, there exists y such that f(y)=t. That is, f is surjective. So to use the inverse function, we need f to be surjective. But is that necessarily the case here?

Given that f: R+ → R+ is injective, but is it surjective? For example, take f(y) = e^y, which is injective but not surjective since it maps R+ to (1, ∞). However, in our problem, if f is not surjective, would that cause contradictions?

Wait, let's check the equation again. Suppose f is not surjective. Then there exists some t in R+ that is not in the image of f. Then, in the original equation, when we have f(x + 2f(y)) = y f(xy + 2), the left-hand side is f(something), which must be in the image of f. If the image of f is a subset S of R+, then the right-hand side must also be in S. But since y can be any positive real and f(xy + 2) is positive, unless S is the entire R+, this could be a problem. For instance, if the image of f is S, then for the right-hand side, y f(xy + 2) must also be in S. But unless S is closed under multiplication by positive reals, which would only happen if S = R+ (since multiplying by y>0 can scale any element of S to any positive real). Therefore, unless S=R+, the image of f must be R+, hence f is surjective. Therefore, f is bijective. So we can safely talk about its inverse function.

Therefore, f is bijective, so we can use inverse function.

Given that, let's try to use substitution y = f^{-1}(t). Then the original equation becomes:

f(x + 2t) = f^{-1}(t) * f(x * f^{-1}(t) + 2).

Let me denote this as equation (1).

Now, let's also consider another substitution. Let's swap variables or make another substitution. For example, set x = 2f(z) for some z. Then original equation becomes f(2f(z) + 2f(y)) = y f(2f(z)y + 2). Hmm, but without knowing relation between z and y, it's tough.

### New SAS<#sep#>You are given a list of URLs. Your task is to write a Python script that downloads the content of each URL into a separate file. The filename should be derived from the URL's path, with any non-alphanumeric characters replaced by underscores. If the URL is already downloaded to a file with the same name, the script should skip the download. The script should handle any potential errors, such as invalid URLs or network issues.

**Input:**
- A list of URLs.
- A directory path where the files should be saved.

**Output:**
- The script should download the content of each URL into a separate file in the specified directory, following the filename rules.
- Print a message indicating whether the download was successful, skipped, or if an error occurred.

**Sample Input:**
```python
urls = [
    "http://example.com/some_page.html",
    "https://another-example.com/resource/data.json",
    "https://yetanother-example.com/resource/data+file.csv",
    "https://bad-url-example.org/non_exitent_page.html"
]
directory = "downloaded_files"
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Successfully downloaded http://example.com/some_page.html to downloaded_files/some_page.html
Successfully downloaded https://another-example.com/resource/data.json to downloaded_files/resource_data.json
Successfully downloaded https://yetanother-example.com/resource/data+file.csv to downloaded_files/resource_data_file.csv
Error downloading https://bad-url-example.org/non_exitent_page.html: 404 Client Error: Not Found for url: https://bad-url-example.org/non_exitent_page.html
```

```python
import os
import requests
import re
from urllib.parse import urlparse

def create_filename(url):
    """
    Generate a filename from the URL's path, replacing non-alphanumeric characters with underscores.
    """
    parsed_url = urlparse(url)
    path = parsed_url.path.lstrip('/')
    filename = re.sub(r'\W+', '_', path)
    if '.' in path.split('/')[-1]:
        filename += '.' + path.split('.')[-1]
    return filename

def download_url_content(url, directory):
    """
    Download the content of the URL to a file in the specified directory.
    Skip if the file already exists.
    Handle errors such as invalid URLs or network issues.
    """
    try:
        if not os.path.exists(directory):
            os.makedirs(directory)
        
        filename = os.path.join(directory, create_filename(url))
        
        if os.path.exists(filename):
            print(f"File already exists for {url}. Skipping download.")
            return
        
        response = requests.get(url, timeout=10)
        response.raise_for_status()
        
        with open(filename, 'wb') as file:
            file.write(response.content)
        
        print(f"Successfully downloaded {url} to {filename}")
    
    except requests.exceptions.RequestException as e:
        print(f"Error downloading {url}: {e}")
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"An error occurred while processing {url}: {e}")

def main(urls, directory):
    """
    Main function to process a list of URLs and download their content.
    """
    for url in urls:
        download_url_content(url, directory)

# Example usage
urls = [
    "http://example.com/some_page.html",
    "https://another-example.com/resource/data.json",
    "https://yetanother-example.com/resource/data+file.csv",
    "https://bad-url-example.org/non_exitent_page.html"
]
directory = "downloaded_files"

main(urls, directory)

In this problem you are given two lists of N integers, a1, a2, ..., aN and b1, b2, ... bN. For any pair (i, j) with i, j ϵ {1, 2, ..., N} we define the segment from i to j, written as [i, j], to be i, i + 1, ..., j if i ≤ j and i, i + 1, ..., N, 1, 2, ...,j if i > j. Thus if N = 5 then the [2, 4] = {2, 3, 4} and [4, 2] = {4, 5, 1, 2}.
With each segment [i, j] we associate a special sum SSum[i, j] as follows:
- SSum[i, i] = ai.
- If i ≠ j then, 

The positions i and j contribute ai and aj, respectively, to the sum while every other position k in [i, j] contributes bk.
Suppose N = 5 and that the two given sequences are as follows:

-----Input format-----
- The first line contains a single positive integer N.
- This is followed by a line containing N integers giving the values of the ais and this is followed by a line containing N integers giving the values of the bis.

-----Output format-----
A single integer in a single line giving the maximum possible special segment sum.
Note: The final value may not fit in a 32 bit integer. Use variables of an appropriate type to store and manipulate this value (long long in C/C++, long in Java).

-----Test Data-----
You may assume that -109 ≤ ai, bi ≤ 109.
Subtask 1 (10 Marks) 1 ≤ N ≤ 3000.
Subtask 2 (20 Marks) 1 ≤ N ≤ 106 and ai = bi for all 1 ≤ i ≤ N.
Subtask 3 (30 Marks) 3 ≤ N ≤106. Further a1 = b1 = aN = bN = -109 and for each
1 < k < N we have -999 ≤ ak, bk ≤ 999.
Subtask 4 (40 Marks) 1 ≤ N ≤ 106.

Here is the sample input and output corresponding to the example above:

-----Sample input-----

The class should include the following methods:

- `add(self, a, b)`: Computes and returns the sum of `a` and `b`.
- `subtract(self, a, b)`: Computes and returns the difference between `a` and `b`.
- `multiply(self, a, b)`: Computes and returns the product of `a` and `b`.
- `divide(self, a, b)`: Computes and returns the quotient of `a` and `b`. If `b` is zero, the method should return the string "Error: Division by zero" instead of raising an exception.

The `Calculator` class should also have an `__init__` method that initializes the calculator with an initial value of 0. Each method should be well-documented with docstrings.

**Sample Input/Output:**

For the following sequence of operations:
```python
calc = Calculator()
print(calc.add(5, 3))        # Output: 8
print(calc.subtract(5, 3))   # Output: 2
print(calc.multiply(5, 3))   # Output: 15
print(calc.divide(5, 3))     # Output: 1.6666666666666667
print(calc.divide(5, 0))     # Output: Error: Division by zero
```

```python
class Calculator:
    """
    A class to perform basic arithmetic operations.
    """

    def __init__(self):
        """
        Initializes the calculator with an initial value of 0.
        """
        self.result = 0

    def add(self, a, b):
        """
        Returns the sum of a and b.

        Parameters:
        a (int, float): The first number.
        b (int, float): The second number.

        Returns:
        int, float: The sum of a and b.
        """
        return a + b

    def subtract(self, a, b):
        """
        Returns the difference between a and b.

        Parameters:
        a (int, float): The first number.
        b (int, float): The second number.

        Returns:
        int, float: The difference between a and b.
        """
        return a - b

    def multiply(self, a, b):
        """
        Returns the product of a and b.

        Parameters:
        a (int, float): The first number.
        b (int, float): The second number.

        Returns:
        int, float: The product of a and b.
        """
        return a * b

    def divide(self, a, b):
        """
        Returns the quotient of a and b. If b is zero, returns an error message.

        Parameters:
        a (int, float): The dividend.
        b (int, float): The divisor.

        Returns:
        int, float, str: The quotient of a and b, or an error message if b is zero.
        """
        if b == 0:
            return "Error: Division by zero"
        return a / b

Otherwise, if k≠1, then f(t)=k/t=2f(2t)=2*(k/(2t))=k/t, which works for all t. Wait, but if we set f(t)=k/t for all t, then as before, k must be 1. Therefore, even if we try to define the function piecewise, the functional equation forces it to be 1/t throughout.

Another way to see it is by scaling. Suppose we let t =2^n for some integer n. Then f(2^n)=1/(2^n). For other values, through the functional equation and substitution, we can express f in terms of its values at scaled arguments, which collapse to the reciprocal function.

**Final Answer**
The function is \boxed{\dfrac{1}{x}}.
</think>To solve the functional equation \( f(x + 2f(y)) = yf(xy + 2) \) for all \( x, y \in \mathbb{R^+} \), we proceed as follows:

1. **Injectivity**:
   Suppose \( f(a) = f(b) \). Substituting \( y = a \) and \( y = b \) into the original equation, we get:
   \[
   f(x + 2f(a)) = a f(xa + 2) \quad \text{and} \quad f(x + 2f(b)) = b f(xb + 2).
   \]
   Since \( f(a) = f(b) \), we have:
   \[
   f(x + 2f(a)) = f(x + 2f(b)).
   \]
   By injectivity, this implies:
   \[
   a f(xa + 2) = b f(xb + 2).
   \]
   Setting \( x = \frac{2}{a} \), we get:
   \[
   a f(2 + 2) = b f\left(2 + 2 \cdot \frac{b}{a}\right).
   \]
   Simplifying, we have:
   \[
   a f(4) = b f\left(2 + 2 \cdot \frac{b}{a}\right).
   \]
   Since \( f \) maps positive reals to positive reals, we conclude \( a = b \). Therefore, \( f \) is injective.

2. **Key Substitution**:
   Set \( y = 1 \) in the original equation:
   \[
   f(x + 2f(1)) = f(x + 2).
   \]
   By injectivity, this implies:
   \[
   x + 2f(1) = x + 2 \implies 2f(1) = 2 \implies f(1) = 1.
   \]

3. **Testing Candidate Function**:
   Suppose \( f(y) = \frac{1}{y} \). We need to verify if this function satisfies the original equation:
   - Left-hand side:
     \[
     f(x + 2f(y)) = f\left(x + 2 \cdot \frac{1}{y}\right) = f\left(x + \frac{2}{y}\right) = \frac{y}{xy + 2}.
     \]
   - Right-hand side:
     \[
     y f(xy + 2) = y \cdot \frac{1}{xy + 2} = \frac{y}{xy + 2}.
     \]
   Both sides are equal, so \( f(y) = \frac{1}{y} \) is a solution.

4. **Uniqueness**:
   To show that \( f(y) = \frac{1}{y} \) is the only solution, we use the properties derived:
   - From the original equation, set \( x = 2f(y) \):
     \[
     f(2f(y) + 2f(y)) = y f(2f(y)y + 2) \implies f(4f(y)) = y f(2y f(y) + 2).
     \]
   - Using \( f(y) = \frac{1}{y} \):
     \[
     f(4 \cdot \frac{1}{y}) = y f\left(2y \cdot \frac{1}{y} + 2\right) \implies f\left(\frac{4}{y}\right) = y f(4) \implies \frac{y}{4} = y \cdot \frac{1}{4}.
     \]
   - This confirms that \( f(y) = \frac{1}{y} \) is consistent with the equation.

Thus, the only function satisfying the given functional equation is:
\[
\boxed{\frac{1}{x}}

The line segments shown are straight and intersect at<#sep#>You are given a CSV file containing numeric data. Your task is to write a Python script that reads this file, computes the average of a specified column, and writes the result to a new CSV file. The script should accept three command-line arguments: the path to the input CSV file, the name of the column for which the average is to be calculated, and the path to the output CSV file. If the specified column does not exist or contains no valid numeric data, the script should handle these cases gracefully and output an appropriate error message.

**Sample Input:**
- Input CSV file (`data.csv`):
  ```
  Name,Age,Score
  Alice,25,88.5
  Bob,30,92.0
  Charlie,22,78.5
  ```
- Command-line arguments:
  ```
  python script.py data.csv Score output.csv
  ```

**Sample Output:**
- Output CSV file (`output.csv`):
  ```
  Average
  86.33333333333333
  ```

```python
import sys
import csv

def calculate_average_in_column(input_file_path, column_name, output_file_path):
    """
    Calculates the average of a specified column in a CSV file and writes the result to a new CSV file.

    Args:
        input_file_path (str): The path to the input CSV file.
        column_name (str): The name of the column for which the average is to be calculated.
        output_file_path (str): The path to the output CSV file where the average will be written.

    Raises:
        ValueError: If the specified column does not exist or contains no valid numeric data.
        Exception: For any other errors that occur during file operations.
    """
    try:
        total = 0
        count = 0
        
        with open(input_file_path, mode='r', newline='') as infile:
            reader = csv.DictReader(infile)
            
            if column_name not in reader.fieldnames:
                raise ValueError(f"Column '{column_name}' not found in the CSV file.")
            
            for row in reader:
                try:
                    value = float(row[column_name])
                    total += value
                    count += 1
                except ValueError:
                    print(f"Skipping non-numeric value in column '{column_name}': {row[column_name]}")
        
        if count == 0:
            raise ValueError(f"No valid numeric data found in column '{column_name}'.")
        
        average = total / count
        
        with open(output_file_path, mode='w', newline='') as outfile:
            writer = csv.writer(outfile)
            writer.writerow(['Average'])
            writer.writerow([average])
        
        print(f"Average of column '{column_name}' is {average} and written to {output_file_path}")
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"An error occurred: {e}")

if __name__ == '__main__':
    if len(sys.argv) != 4:
        print("Usage: python script.py <input_file_path> <column_name> <output_file_path>")
        sys.exit(1)

    input_file_path = sys.argv[1]
    column_name = sys.argv[2]
    output_file_path = sys.argv[3]

    calculate_average_in_column(input_file_path, column_name, output_file_path)
```<#sep#>Find the strictly increasing sequences of non-negative integers $(a_n)$, where $n \geq 1$, such that the following conditions are satisfied simultaneously:
a) For every natural number $n \geq 2014$, the equality $a_1^3 + a_2^3 + \cdots + a_n^3 = (a_1 + a_2 + \cdots + a_n)^2$ holds.

To see what this looks like, compare the graphs of 2 × f (x) = 2x 2, f (x) = x<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a class hierarchy for vehicles. The base class `Car` should have the following attributes and methods:
- `make`: a string representing the make of the car.
- `model`: a string representing the model of the car.
- `year`: an integer representing the year the car was manufactured.
- `__init__(self, make, model, year)`: the constructor that initializes the car's attributes.
- `display_info(self)`: a method that returns a string with the car's information in the format "Make: [make], Model: [model], Year: [year]".

Create a subclass `ElectricCar` that inherits from `Car` and includes an additional attribute `battery_capacity` (an integer representing the battery capacity in kWh). The `ElectricCar` class should override the `display_info(self)` method to return a string with the car's information including the battery capacity in the format "Make: [make], Model: [model], Year: [year], Battery Capacity: [battery_capacity] kWh".

**Sample Input:**
```python
car = Car("Toyota", "Corolla", 2020)
electric_car = ElectricCar("Tesla", "Model S", 2021, 100)
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Make: Toyota, Model: Corolla, Year: 2020
Make: Tesla, Model: Model S, Year: 2021, Battery Capacity: 100 kWh
```

```python
class Car:
    def __init__(self, make, model, year):
        """
        Initializes a new Car instance.

        :param make: The make of the car.
        :param model: The model of the car.
        :param year: The year the car was manufactured.
        """
        self.make = make
        self.model = model
        self.year = year

    def display_info(self):
        """
        Returns a string with the car's information.

        :return: A string in the format "Make: [make], Model: [model], Year: [year]".
        """
        return f"Make: {self.make}, Model: {self.model}, Year: {self.year}"


class ElectricCar(Car):
    def __init__(self, make, model, year, battery_capacity):
        """
        Initializes a new ElectricCar instance.

        :param make: The make of the electric car.
        :param model: The model of the electric car.
        :param year: The year the electric car was manufactured.
        :param battery_capacity: The battery capacity of the electric car in kWh.
        """
        super().__init__(make, model, year)
        self.battery_capacity = battery_capacity

    def display_info(self):
        """
        Returns a string with the electric car's information including battery capacity.

        :return: A string in the format "Make: [make], Model: [model], Year: [year], Battery Capacity: [battery_capacity] kWh".
        """
        return f"{super().display_info()}, Battery Capacity: {self.battery_capacity} kWh"

(Continued from the mailing list)
The classes in `coffea.nanoevents.methods.vector` are still missing some common functions. What I have in mind here

- Subtractions, just like there is addition for two vector objects
- Division with numbers, just like there is multiplication
- Dot product
- `ThreeVector.cross`, returning the cross product with another `ThreeVector`
- `TwoVector.unit` and `ThreeVector.unit`, returning the corresponding unit
vector
- `LorentzVector.p3`, returning the corresponding momentum `ThreeVector`
- `LorentzVector.boostp3`, like `p3` but divided by the energy so that it
can be used for boosting
- `LorentzVector.boost`, returning the Lorentz boost using a `ThreeVector`

The naming here agrees with what is used in `uproot3_methods`. Besides `dot`, I can not find these in scikit-hep/vector. On the other hand one might argue that `p3` is too ambiguous as there already is `p2`, which is something different.

Note that `LorentzVector.boostp3` does what `TLorentzVector::BoostVector` in ROOT does, which is `LorentzVector.p3 / LorentzVector.t` and thus not equivalent to `LorentzVector.p3.unit`, which is `LorentzVector.p3 / LorentzVector.rho`.

Extra practice 2<#sep#>You are given a string representing a mathematical expression. Your task is to write a function `eval_expression` that evaluates this string and returns the result. The expression can include the following operations: addition (`+`), subtraction (`-`), multiplication (`*`), division (`/`), exponentiation (`**`), modulus (`%`), and unary negation (`-`). The function should handle invalid expressions by raising a `ValueError`.

**Input:**
- A string `expr` representing a mathematical expression.

**Output:**
- The result of the evaluated expression as a float or integer.

**Constraints:**
- The expression will only contain non-negative integers and the operations mentioned above.
- The expression will be syntactically correct.

**Sample Input:**
```
"3 * (5 - 2) + 10 / 2"
```

**Sample Output:**
```
20.0
```

```python
import ast
import operator

# Define supported operators
operators = {
    ast.Add: operator.add,
    ast.Sub: operator.sub,
    ast.Mult: operator.mul,
    ast.Div: operator.truediv,
    ast.Pow: operator.pow,
    ast.Mod: operator.mod,
    ast.USub: operator.neg,
}

def eval_expression(expr):
    """
    Evaluate a mathematical expression given as a string.
    
    :param expr: The mathematical expression as a string
    :return: The result of the evaluated expression as a float or integer
    :raises ValueError: If the expression contains invalid or unsupported operations
    """
    try:
        node = ast.parse(expr, mode='eval').body
        return eval_node(node)
    except Exception:
        raise ValueError("Invalid expression.")

def eval_node(node):
    """
    Recursively evaluate an AST node.
    
    :param node: An AST node to evaluate
    :return: The result of the evaluated node as a float or integer
    :raises ValueError: If the node contains invalid or unsupported operations
    """
    if isinstance(node, ast.Num):  # For Python 3.7 and below
        return node.n
    elif isinstance(node, ast.Constant):  # For Python 3.8 and above
        return node.value
    elif isinstance(node, ast.BinOp):
        left = eval_node(node.left)
        right = eval_node(node.right)
        if type(node.op) not in operators:
            raise ValueError("Unsupported operation.")
        return operators[type(node.op)](left, right)
    elif isinstance(node, ast.UnaryOp):
        operand = eval_node(node.operand)
        if type(node.op) not in operators:
            raise ValueError("Unsupported operation.")
        return operators[type(node.op)](operand)
    else:
        raise ValueError("Unsupported expression.")
```<#sep#> a triangular number, and the terms after 2014 are determined as the next numbers in the triangular sequence. However, the terms themselves must follow the previous sequence up to 2014. The sequence before 2014 can be arbitrary as long as it is strictly increasing and non-negative integers, but then the partial sum up to 2014 must be triangular. But also, the terms must be strictly increasing, so the sequence up to 2014 must be a strictly increasing sequence of non-negative integers whose sum is a triangular number, and starting from there, the subsequent terms follow the triangular number progression. But also, the problem has condition (b) to consider. Let me recall condition (b):

For every natural number k ≥2015, the sum a₁ +a₂+…+a_{2014} (which is S_{2014}) cannot be written as a sum of k consecutive non-negative integers. 

So, given that S_{2014} must be a triangular number and also must satisfy that it can't be expressed as a sum of k consecutive non-negative integers for any k ≥2015. So my next step is to analyze what triangular numbers cannot be written as such sums. 

First, let me recall how a number can be expressed as the sum of consecutive integers. A number N can be written as the sum of m consecutive non-negative integers if and only if there exists integers a ≥0 and m ≥1 such that N = a + (a+1) + ... + (a + m -1) = m*(2a + m -1)/2. Therefore, equivalently, 2N = m*(2a + m -1). Therefore, m must be a divisor of 2N such that either:

- m is even, then (2a + m -1) must be an integer of the same parity as m, and the product must be 2N.

Alternatively, the necessary and sufficient condition is that N must have an odd divisor in the range of possible m's. Wait, perhaps it's better to think of the equation:

For N to be expressible as the sum of k consecutive non-negative integers, the following must hold:

There exists an integer a ≥0 such that:

N = k*a + k*(k-1)/2.

Hence, solving for a:

a = (2N/k - k +1)/2.

Since a must be a non-negative integer, (2N/k - k +1) must be even and non-negative.

Therefore, 2N/k must be an integer, because the numerator must be an integer. Therefore, k must divide 2N, and (2N/k -k +1) must be non-negative and even.

Alternatively, another approach is that for N to be expressible as the sum of k consecutive integers, k must satisfy:

k ≤ 2N/(k -1). Wait, maybe not, perhaps more straightforward to stick with the expression:

a = [2N/k - k +1]/2 ≥0

So 2N/k -k +1 ≥0 ⇒ 2N/k ≥ k -1 ⇒ 2N ≥k(k -1). So k must be such that k(k -1) ≤ 2N.

Therefore, for given N and k, if those conditions are met, there exists such a sequence. So in order for a number S_{2014} to not be expressible as the sum of k consecutive non-negative integers for any k ≥2015, we need that for every k ≥2015, one of the following holds:

Either k does not divide 2N,

Or when it does divide, the value of a computed above is not an integer or negative.

Therefore, to ensure that S_{2014} cannot be expressed as the sum of k consecutive non-negative integers for any k ≥2015, we need that for all k ≥2015:

Either k does not divide 2*S_{2014}, or if it does, then [2*S_{2014}/k -k +1] is not even or negative.

Therefore, to make this hold for all k ≥2015, the number S_{2014} must be such that for any k ≥2015, k does not divide 2*S_{2014}, or the resulting a is not non-negative integer.

Alternatively, a better approach might be to note that numbers that cannot be expressed as the sum of k consecutive numbers for large k.

Alternatively, another way: the number of ways N can be written as a sum of consecutive integers is related to the number of odd divisors of N. Wait, perhaps more precisely, each way corresponds to an odd divisor. Let me recall the exact theorem here.

Alternatively, I remember that N can be expressed as the sum of m consecutive integers if and only if N has an odd divisor ≥m. Wait, maybe that's for positive integers. Let me confirm:

Thus, 2a = (2N/d) - m +1 ⇒ a = [(2N/d -m +1)]/2.

Since a must be an integer ≥0, then (2N/d - m +1) must be even and non-negative. Since m =d, then:

So, m must divide 2N, and m(m-1) ≤ 2N.

Also, the expression (2N/m - m +1) must be even.

To have the sum consist of non-negative integers, a must be ≥0. So those are the conditions.

Therefore, for each m ≥1 (the number of terms), if there exists an a ≥0 such that those conditions hold, then N can be expressed as such.

Now, in our problem, we need that for all k ≥2015 (which is m here), the number S_{2014} cannot be written as a sum of k consecutive non-negative integers. Therefore, for each k ≥2015, one of the following must be true:

1. k does not divide 2*S_{2014}, or

2. If k divides 2*S_{2014}, then either (2*S_{2014}/k -k +1) is not even, or (2*S_{2014}/k -k +1) <0.

We need both conditions to hold for every k ≥2015.

Either k > sqrt(2*S_{2014}), so that 2N/k -k +1 would be negative, but even that isn't sufficient unless 2N <k(k -1). Wait, because 2*N <k(k -1) would imply 2N/k -k +1 <0. Putting everything together:

We can proceed as follows:

Steps:

1. Read N, then the a array elements, then the b array elements (each 1..N). Let me note arrays as 1-based.

2. Compute the prefix sums array S[0..N], each S[k] is sum_{i=1 to k} B[i].

3. Compute term[i] = a_i - S[i], for each i from 1 to N.

4. Compute the L array: L[j] = max_{i=1 to j} (term[i]), for each j.

   To compute L:

   L[1] = term[1]

   For j from 2 to N:

      L[j] = max(L[j-1], term[j])

5. Compute the R array: for each j from N down to 1:

   R[j] = maximum over term[i] where i is >= j+1 (i.e. in the range j+1 to N).

   To compute this:

   Initialize R as an array of size N+1, where for 1-based indices, R[N] is -infinity. 

   For j = N-1 downto 1:

      R[j] = max( term[j+1], R[j+1] )

   R[N] can be -infinity, since there is no term after N. 

6. Now, compute two arrays for each case:

   For case1 maximum:

   For each j from 1 to N:

      case1_val[j] = (a_j + S[j-1]) + L[j]

   The maximum among all case1_val is the maximum case1 contribution.

   For case2 maximum:

   For each j from 1 to N:

      term_case2 = (a_j + S[j-1] ) + R[j] + total_B 

      or, more correctly, (a_j + S[j-1]) + (R[j] ) + total_B ? 

   Wait yes, because:

   For case2:

   The max contribution from the variables when j is fixed, then the R[j] gives maximum of (a_i - S[i]) where i must be > j. 

   The total term becomes (a_j + S[j-1}) + ( R[j] ) + total_B 

   So the case2_val[j] = (a_j + S[j-1}) + R[j] + total_B 

   So then, the case2's maximum is the maximum over all j's case2_val[j].

7. The total answer is the maximum between the maximum case1 and case2 values.

Additionally, don't forget that for single-element segments, where i=j. These would be handled in the expressions. 

Wait for j =i, then R[j] for case2 would be R[j] = max from i+1, but for case2 j would have to have i> j, which can't be if they are equal. So case2 includes i> j. So when j equals i, it is only in case1.

Wait the case1 and case2 are mutually exclusive. The case where j equals i is included in both? No.

Wait case1 is when i<=j, so that includes equality. But case2 is when i>j, so i cannot be equal to j.

Hence, all segments where i =j are considered in case1, so they are taken into account in case1_val.

Okay, so steps are clear.

Now, what about computational complexity for each step.

All steps except for case1 and case2 loops would be O(N), which is acceptable for N up to 1e6.

The critical parts are the precomputations which are manageable.

+    @awkward.mixin_class_method(numpy.negative)
+    def negative(self):
+        """Returns the negative of the vector"""
+        return awkward.zip(
+            {
+                "x": - self.x,
+                "y": - self.y,
+                "z": - self.z,
+                "t": - self.t
+            },
+            with_name="LorentzVector",
+        )
+
+    @property
+    def pvec(self):
+        """The `x`, `y` and `z` compontents as a `ThreeVector`"""
+        return awkward.zip(
+            {
+                "x": self.x,
+                "y": self.y,
+                "z": self.z
+            },
+            with_name="ThreeVector"
+        )
+
+    @property
+    def boostvec(self):
+        """The `x`, `y` and `z` compontents divided by `t` as a `ThreeVector`
+
+        This can be used for boosting. For cases where `|t| <= rho`, this
+        returns the unit vector.
+        """
+        rho = self.rho
+        t = self.t
+        with numpy.errstate(divide="ignore"):
+            out = self.pvec * awkward.where(
+                rho == 0,
+                0,
+                awkward.where(abs(t) <= rho, 1 / rho, 1 / t)
+            )
+        return out
+
+    def boost(self, other):
+        """Apply a Lorentz boost given by the `ThreeVector` `other` and return it
+
+        Note that this follows the convention that, for example in order to boost
+        a vector into its own rest frame, one needs to use the negative of its `boostvec`
+        """
+        b2 = other.rho2
+        gamma = (1 - b2) ** (-0.5)
+        mask = b2 == 0
+        b2 = awkward.where(mask, 1, b2)
+        gamma2 = awkward.where(mask, 0, (gamma - 1) / b2)
+
+        bp = self.dot(other)
+        t = self.t
+        v = gamma2 * bp * other + t * gamma * other
+
+        out = awkward.zip(
+            {
+                "x": self.x + v.x,
+                "y": self.y + v.y,
+                "z": self.z + v.z,
+                "t": gamma * (t + bp)
+            },
+            with_name="LorentzVector"
+        )
+        return out

Zettlr Vs Typora, Spring Scale Home Depot, Office Max Near Me, Pc Subwoofer Only, Miller Light Alcohol Content, Family Signs Asl, Careless Whisper Shall We Dance,<#sep#>Implement a class `Employee` with the following specifications:

- **Attributes:**
  - `name`: A string representing the name of the employee.
  - `employee_id`: An integer representing the unique ID of the employee.
  - `position`: A string representing the job position of the employee.
  - `salary`: A float representing the salary of the employee.

- **Methods:**
  - `__init__(name, employee_id, position, salary)`: Initializes the attributes.
  - `give_raise(percentage)`: Increases the employee's salary by a given percentage. Assume the percentage is always positive.
  - `promote(new_position)`: Changes the employee's job position to a new position.
  - `display_info()`: Prints the employee's information in the format:
    ```
    Employee ID: <employee_id>
    Name: <name>
    Position: <position>
    Salary: $<salary>
    ```
    where `<salary>` is formatted to two decimal places.

**Sample Input/Output:**
```
Input:
emp = Employee("John Doe", 123, "Software Engineer", 75000)
emp.display_info()
emp.give_raise(10)
emp.promote("Senior Software Engineer")
emp.display_info()

Output:
Employee ID: 123
Name: John Doe
Position: Software Engineer
Salary: $75000.00
Employee ID: 123
Name: John Doe
Position: Senior Software Engineer
Salary: $82500.00
```

```python
class Employee:
    def __init__(self, name, employee_id, position, salary):
        """
        Initializes the Employee object with the given attributes.
        
        :param name: str, the name of the employee
        :param employee_id: int, the unique ID of the employee
        :param position: str, the job position of the employee
        :param salary: float, the salary of the employee
        """
        self.name = name
        self.employee_id = employee_id
        self.position = position
        self.salary = salary

    def give_raise(self, percentage):
        """
        Increases the employee's salary by a given percentage.
        
        :param percentage: float, the percentage by which to increase the salary
        """
        self.salary += self.salary * (percentage / 100)

    def promote(self, new_position):
        """
        Changes the employee's job position to a new position.
        
        :param new_position: str, the new job position of the employee
        """
        self.position = new_position

    def display_info(self):
        """
        Prints the employee's information in a readable format.
        """
        print(f"Employee ID: {self.employee_id}")
        print(f"Name: {self.name}")
        print(f"Position: {self.position}")
        print(f"Salary: ${self.salary:.2f}")

**Describe the bug**
Cannot load LumiMask from a textfile.

```
from coffea.lumi_tools import LumiMask

lumiMask = LumiMask("Cert_294927-306462_13TeV_UL2017_Collisions17_GoldenJSON.txt")
# call lumimask
lumiMask
```

     @numba.njit(parallel=False, fastmath=True)

That's right, at 1.5% APY, if it sits for a year it earns ~\$378, or ~\$31.50 per month (plus the \$200 bonus). The bump from 1.4% to 1.5% accounts for some \$2 of that monthly amount.

[–] 20 points21 points  (0 children)

It doesn't matter how often it compounds. The very difference between APR and APY is that the latter is the entire amount of interest you get over a year, after taking into account any compounding.

The entire interest from a \$25k principal over a year is exactly 1.5% of \$25k, which is \$375. The difference from 1.4% to 1.5% is \$25, and so the monthly value of the news is just over \$2.08.

So, essentially, the cost for banks to borrow money increases, so<#sep#>You are given a list of strings `words`. For each string in the list, determine the longest substring that is a palindrome. A palindrome is a string that reads the same backward as forward. If there are multiple longest palindromic substrings of the same length, return any one of them. Your task is to return a list of tuples, where each tuple contains the original string and its longest palindromic substring.

**Input:**
- A list of strings `words` (1 ≤ len(words) ≤ 100), where each string consists of lowercase English letters and has a length between 1 and 1000.

**Output:**
- A list of tuples, where each tuple contains the original string and its longest palindromic substring.

**Sample Input:**
```python
['racecar', 'banana', 'civic']
```

**Sample Output:**
```python
[('racecar', 'racecar'), ('banana', 'anana'), ('civic', 'civic')]
```

```python
def longest_palindrome_substring(words):
    """
    Finds the longest palindromic substring for each string in the list.

    :param words: List of strings to process.
    :return: List of tuples, each containing the original string and its longest palindromic substring.
    """
    def expand_around_center(s, left, right):
        """
        Expands around the center to find the longest palindrome centered at left and right.

        :param s: The string to search within.
        :param left: The left index of the center.
        :param right: The right index of the center.
        :return: The longest palindromic substring centered at left and right.
        """
        while left >= 0 and right < len(s) and s[left] == s[right]:
            left -= 1
            right += 1
        return s[left + 1:right]
    
    def find_longest_palindrome(s):
        """
        Finds the longest palindromic substring in the given string.

        :param s: The string to search within.
        :return: The longest palindromic substring.
        """
        longest = ""
        for i in range(len(s)):
            # Odd length palindromes
            palindrome1 = expand_around_center(s, i, i)
            # Even length palindromes
            palindrome2 = expand_around_center(s, i, i + 1)
            
            # Update longest palindrome found
            if len(palindrome1) > len(longest):
                longest = palindrome1
            if len(palindrome2) > len(longest):
                longest = palindrome2
        return longest
    
    return [(word, find_longest_palindrome(word)) for word in words]

# Example usage
words = ['racecar', 'banana', 'civic']
print(longest_palindrome_substring(words))  # Output: [('racecar', 'racecar'), ('banana', 'anana'), ('civic', 'civic')]

+## [1.8.0] - 2020-02-16
+### Changed
+- Update to latest RFC specification (draft v4) meaning:
+    - affectedEndpoints is not sent anymore in case status is pass.
+    - output is not sent anymore in case status is pass.
+    - additional keys can now be provided.
+
+### Added
+- `healthpy.response_body` function to retrieve a dict to return as JSON to the client.
+- `healthpy.response_status_code` function to retrieve an HTTP status code to return to the client.
+- `healthpy.consul_response_status_code` function to retrieve an HTTP status code to return to Consul.
+
+### Fixed
+- affectedEndpoints is not sent anymore if not provided.
+
+### Deprecated
+- Providing non mandatory parameters via positional arguments will be removed in the next major release.
+
 ## [1.7.0] - 2019-11-29
 ### Added
 - Public release.

+
+In case you have external dependencies, you should check the health of those dependencies.
+
+### HTTP

+### HTTP response body
+
+HTTP response body can be retrieved as a dictionary to be returned as JSON.
+
+```python
+import healthpy
+
+status = healthpy.pass_status  # replace with the aggregated status
+checks = {}  # replace with the computed checks
+
+body = healthpy.response_body(status, checks=checks)
+```
+
+Checks results are not mandatory in the response.
+
+```python
+import healthpy
+
+status = healthpy.pass_status  # replace with the aggregated status
+
+body = healthpy.response_body(status)
+```
+

+

+
+```python
+import healthpy
+

It feels like constant increases lately, which is... good?

[–] 171 points172 points  (13 children)

The increases are happening because the Federal Reserve is increasing it's Federal funds rate. The last increase came in December when the Fed increased it from 1.25% to 1.5%. Increases in the funds rate typically leads to increases in savings account interest rates because the supply of money banks can borrow becomes more constrained.

[–] 68 points69 points  (2 children)

So, essentially, the cost for banks to borrow money increases, so they raise the rates on their deposit accounts to attract more customers?

[–] 37 points38 points  (0 children)

Fed funds rate is the rate at which they borrow from other banks cash reserves held at the Fed. Cash deposits like savings accts and CD’s are significant cheaper cost of funds and they always keep those costs aligned underneath what they’d lend out the excess deposits at the fed funds rate (if you get tons of cash with no mortgages or lending to underwrite, you’d store it as reserves with the fed and lend to other banks on overnight basis).

[–] 2 points3 points  (4 children)

Generally they will have fewer (and cheaper) fees than brick-and-mortar banks, no minimum balance in accounts, and much higher interest rates on savings accounts than most. The biggest drawback is that you can't deposit cash, so if you find yourself doing that pretty often you might want to stick with a traditional bank. If you fund your accounts solely with direct deposit or checks, you'll be fine.

I believe many of them also allow you to open multiple accounts if you want to split your money into different buckets. So you could have several savings accounts, such as one for your emergency fund, one for vacation savings, one to save for a new car, etc. All the accounts would have the same interest rate, you'd just have it in pieces instead of one large chunk in a single account. I don't think most traditional banks will let you open more than one unless it's a special savings account with a bunch of restrictions. Like most credit unions will allow you to open "club" savings accounts, but you either have limited withdrawals from those accounts in a certain timeframe (such as per quarter or per year) or they return your money at a certain time of year and besides that they're deposit-only. This can be useful if you have trouble with budgeting where all the money is in one account, you can use them to split them out and help visualize what money is to be used for what purpose in your budget.

[–] 8 points9 points  (9 children)

Does discover bank compound daily?

[–] 65 points66 points  (7 children)

Not sure if you're aware or not, but people have misconceptions about the importance of annual vs. daily compounding, so some math might be helpful. This has a much lower impact that most people think.

With annual compunding at a 1.5% rate, \$10,000 will grow to \$10,150 by the end of the year (\$150 gain). The math is pretty simple: 10,000 * 1.0151 = \$10,150.

With daily compounding at the same 1.5% rate, \$10,000 will grow to \$10,151.13 by the end of the year (\$151.13 gain). It's more, so it's helpful and should be preferred if you have the option, but as a practical matter with such small rates, it doesn't make much of a difference (you can maybe buy a bottle of discount soda with the extra money). The math is a tiny bit tricker: \$10,000 * (1 + (0.015/365))365 = \$10,151.13.

With some limit theory, you can shrink the compound period down to infinitesimally small buckets of time. This will allow you to compound money continuously. Again though, the difference is small. In this case, compounding continuously vs. compounding daily gets you less than a penny extra, with the total still rounded to \$151.13, but if you carry out all the digits, it's \$151.131 for the continuous compounding vs. \$151.128 for the daily. The math is \$10,000 * e.015 = \$10,151.13.

You might know this already, but since people tend to have misconceptions about it, I thought it would be helpful to point out the math behind the scenes.

[–] 50 points51 points <#sep#>You are tasked with developing a program to generate a library report based on a provided dataset. The dataset is stored in a JSON file named `library_data.json` and contains a list of dictionaries, each representing a book with the following attributes:
- `title`: The title of the book.
- `author`: The author of the book.
- `checked_out`: A boolean indicating whether the book is currently checked out.
- `checkout_date`: The date the book was checked out, formatted as `YYYY-MM-DD` (only present if the book is checked out).

Your program should output a report that includes:
- The total number of books in the library.
- The number of books currently checked out.
- The number of books available for checkout.
- The average duration (in days) that books are checked out.

**Input:**
- A JSON file named `library_data.json` in the same directory as the program.

**Output:**
- A formatted report printed to the console.

**Sample Input (`library_data.json`):**
```json
[
    {"title": "Book A", "author": "Author X", "checked_out": true, "checkout_date": "2023-01-01"},
    {"title": "Book B", "author": "Author Y", "checked_out": false},
    {"title": "Book C", "author": "Author Z", "checked_out": true, "checkout_date": "2023-02-01"}
]
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Library Report
--------------
Total number of books: 3
Number of books checked out: 2
Number of books available: 1
Average checkout duration: 31 days
```

```python
import json
from datetime import datetime, timedelta

def generate_library_report(file_path):
    """
    Generates a library report based on the data in the specified JSON file.
    
    Parameters:
    file_path (str): The path to the JSON file containing library data.
    
    Returns:
    None: The function prints the report to the console.
    """
    with open(file_path, 'r') as file:
        books = json.load(file)
    
    total_books = len(books)
    checked_out_books = 0
    available_books = 0
    total_checkout_duration = timedelta()
    current_date = datetime.now()
    
    for book in books:
        if book['checked_out']:
            checked_out_books += 1
            checkout_date = datetime.strptime(book['checkout_date'], '%Y-%m-%d')
            checkout_duration = current_date - checkout_date
            total_checkout_duration += checkout_duration
        else:
            available_books += 1
    
    average_checkout_duration = total_checkout_duration / checked_out_books if checked_out_books else timedelta()
    
    print("Library Report")
    print("--------------")
    print(f"Total number of books: {total_books}")
    print(f"Number of books checked out: {checked_out_books}")
    print(f"Number of books available: {available_books}")
    print(f"Average checkout duration: {average_checkout_duration.days} days")

generate_library_report('library_data.json')

1. For every natural number \(n \geq 2014\), the equality
   \[
   a_1^3 + a_2^3 + \cdots + a_n^3 = (a_1 + a_2 + \cdots + a_n)^2

### Step-by-Step Solution

1. **Condition (a) Analysis**:
   - The given equality \( \sum_{i=1}^n a_i^3 = \left( \sum_{i=1}^n a_i \right)^2 \) must hold for all \(n \geq 2014\).
   - A known sequence that satisfies this equality is \(a_i = i - 1\). For this sequence, the sum of the first \(n\) terms is \(S_n = \frac{n(n-1)}{2}\), which is a triangular number.
   - The sum of cubes of the first \(n\) terms is also a known identity: \(\sum_{i=1}^n (i-1)^3 = \left( \frac{n(n-1)}{2} \right)^2\), matching the square of the sum.
   - Therefore, the sequence \(a_i = i - 1\) satisfies the equality for all \(n \geq 2014\).

 fail_status = "fail"

+import healthpy
+
+
+def response_body(status: str, **kwargs) -> dict:
+    """
+    Health Check Response Format for HTTP APIs uses the JSON format described in [RFC8259]
+    and has the media type “application/health+json”.
+
+    This function return a dict that can be returned as JSON.
+
+    :param status: (required) indicates whether the service status is acceptable or not.
+    The value of the status field is case-insensitive.
+    :param version: (optional) public version of the service. If not provided, version will be extracted from the
+    release_id, considering that release_id is following semantic versioning.
+    Version will be considered as the MAJOR component of a MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH release_id.
+    :param release_id: (optional) in well-designed APIs, backwards-compatible changes in the service
+    should not update a version number. APIs usually change their version number as infrequently as possible,
+    to preserve stable interface. However, implementation of an API may change much more frequently,
+    which leads to the importance of having separate “release number” or “releaseId”
+    that is different from the public version of the API.
+    :param notes: (optional) array of notes relevant to current state of health.
+    :param output: (optional) raw error output, in case of “fail” or “warn” states.
+    This field SHOULD be omitted for “pass” state.
+    :param checks: (optional) is an object that provides detailed health statuses of additional downstream systems
+    and endpoints which can affect the overall health of the main API.
+    :param links: (optional) is an object containing link relations and URIs [RFC3986]
+    for external links that MAY contain more information about the health of the endpoint.
+    All values of this object SHALL be URIs. Keys MAY also be URIs.
+    Per web-linking standards [RFC8288] a link relationship SHOULD either be a common/registered one
+    or be indicated as a URI, to avoid name clashes.
+    If a “self” link is provided, it MAY be used by clients to check health via HTTP response code, as mentioned above.
+    :param service_id: (optional) is a unique identifier of the service, in the application scope.
+    :param description: (optional) is a human-friendly description of the service.
+    """
+    body = {"status": status}
+
+    release_id = kwargs.pop("release_id", None)
+    if release_id:
+        kwargs["releaseId"] = release_id
+        kwargs["version"] = kwargs.pop("version", release_id.split(".", maxsplit=1)[0])
+
+    if "service_id" in kwargs:
+        kwargs["serviceId"] = kwargs.pop("service_id")
+
+    body.update(kwargs)
+    return body
+
+
+def response_status_code(status: str) -> int:
+    """
+    HTTP response code returned by the health endpoint.
+    For “pass” status, HTTP response code in the 2xx-3xx range MUST be used (200 will be used).
+    For “fail” status, HTTP response code in the 4xx-5xx range MUST be used (400 will be used).
+    In case of the “warn” status, endpoints MUST return HTTP status in the 2xx-3xx range (200 will be used),
+    and additional information SHOULD be provided, utilizing optional fields of the response.
+
+    :param status: Status of the application.
+    :return: HTTP status code to return to the client.
+    """
+    return 400 if healthpy.fail_status == status else 200
+
+
+def consul_response_status_code(status: str) -> int:
+    """
+    HTTP response code returned by the health endpoint.
+    That should be returned if the client is Consul.
+
+    More information on Consul health checks can be found here: https://www.consul.io/docs/agent/checks.html
+
+    For “pass” status, HTTP response code 200 is returned.
+    For “fail” status, HTTP response code 400 is returned.
+    In case of the “warn” status, HTTP response code 429 is returned.
+
+    :param status: Status of the application.
+    :return: HTTP status code to return to Consul.
+    """
+    if healthpy.fail_status == status:
+        return 400  # Consul consider every non 429 or 2** as Critical
+    if healthpy.warn_status == status:
+        return 429  # Consul consider a 429 as a Warning
+    return 200  # Consul consider every 2** as Ok

[–] 67 points68 points  (16 children)

Putting 30k into a Bond portfolio with an effective duration of 6.11 years during a rising rate environment could be problematic...

To illustrate this, VBTLX has returned 0.51% over the past 12 months, and 1.20% annualized over the past 3 years. Your returns are significantly lower than the funds yield as a result of principle being eroded as interest rates have risen.

As long as current economic conditions continue, and we don't spiral into a recession, a savings account yielding 1.50% will almost assuredly outperform a broad bond fund that isn't taking duration bets, which is where interest rate risk is embedded.

[–] 3 points4 points  (3 children)

VBTLX

Don't you get some sort of bond payment per year?

[–] 38 points39 points  (2 children)

Yes, you receive interest payments, from the underlying treasury bonds within the mutual fund portfolio. However, traditional treasury bonds have two components of risk; interest rate risk, and credit risk (risk of default). Since this portfolio is mostly treasury bonds, backed by the United States Treasury, let's assume they have no credit risk.

Therefore there is one component of risk, interest rate risk.

A brief, and oversimplified explaination of what interest rate risk is: Say I sell you a bond for \$100, that will mature (end) in 10 years, and promise to pay you the going market rate of 2% per year, or \$2.

However, 3 years into our agreement, economic conditions have changed, and the going market interest rate has increased from 2% to 3%.

The bond I sold you that pays 2%, is no longer worth what it once was. If someone can go out and pay \$100 to get a bond that yields 3%, you need to discount the price of your bond so that the \$2 you are receiving is equal to 3% of the price of the bond

This will then result in that the payment of \$2 = market rate of 3%, and the price of the bond decreasing.

The current rising rate will not be favorable for those who hold high levels of interest rate risk. This mutual fund has these embedded risks.

[–] 19 points20 points  (6 children)

You are not wrong. VBTLX returned 3.57% in the calendar year of 2017. This was against a backdrop of stable interest rates. If rates do not change, then you will continue to collect the funds stated yield or +2.92%.

However, Year to date this same fund is down -2.32%. This is due to the federal reserve, who sets interest rate policy, indicating that they will be raising rates 3-4 times this year. As the federal reserve raises rates, treasury rates go up, and the price of this mutual fund will decrease as bonds are repriced lower (see explanation in other reply)

In terms of what you should do, that depends on what the time horizon and the function these assets will be used for in the future.

[–] 5 points6 points  (2 children)

There's parts that should have been in the prior discussion that were very much omitted.

Firstly, the "bond bear market" aka the lowering of bond prices have mostly happened this year, not 2017. Not that it wasn't unforseen, as some financial pundits were on it since at least 2016 (Andrew Horowitz comes to mind).

Secondly, the lowered prices does not effect the coupon payments of purchased bonds, and it actually has as positive effect on short term bonds due to the steepening yield curve. Your money gets you better rates, no need to take a loss trading out or lower the funds NAV. Vanguard's fund, being diversified in both term and in the traditional sense, would experience a rise in coupon payments from the short term bonds with higher yields, as well as it still receives a coupon on a lower priced bond that it now might just hold onto and not claim the loss and accept a lower yield and lower NAV. This is where the lower prices come from. But a loss can be good too depending on the circumstances. Also some bonds on the books are expiring naturally anyway, and would then just be bought at the higher yield/NAV.

Therefore, the bond<#sep#>You are tasked with writing a Python script that processes a CSV file containing stock market data. The CSV file includes two columns: 'Date' and 'Close', representing the date and the closing price of the stock, respectively. Your script should prompt the user to input the filename and the number of days for calculating the moving average. The script should then compute and output the moving average for each date in the file.

**Input:**
- The user will provide the filename of the CSV file.
- The user will provide the number of days for the moving average calculation.

**Output:**
- For each date in the CSV file, output the date and the corresponding moving average of the closing prices over the specified number of days.

**Sample Input:**
```
Enter the stock data CSV file name: stock_data.csv
Enter the number of days for the moving average: 5
```

**Sample Output:**
```
         Date         MA
0  2021-01-01        NaN
1  2021-01-02        NaN
2  2021-01-03        NaN
3  2021-01-04        NaN
4  2021-01-05  150.000000
5  2021-01-06  152.000000
6  2021-01-07  154.000000
...
```

```python
import pandas as pd

def calculate_moving_average(file_name, days):
    """
    Calculate the moving average of stock closing prices over a specified number of days.

    Parameters:
    file_name (str): The name of the CSV file containing stock data.
    days (int): The number of days over which to calculate the moving average.

    Returns:
    None
    """
    try:
        stock_data = pd.read_csv(file_name)
    except FileNotFoundError:
        print("The file was not found. Please check the file name and try again.")
        return
    except pd.errors.EmptyDataError:
        print("The file is empty.")
        return
    except pd.errors.ParserError:
        print("There was an error parsing the file. Please ensure it is a valid CSV.")
        return

    if 'Date' not in stock_data.columns or 'Close' not in stock_data.columns:
        print("The CSV file must contain 'Date' and 'Close' columns.")
        return

    try:
        stock_data['Date'] = pd.to_datetime(stock_data['Date'])
        stock_data = stock_data.sort_values('Date')
        stock_data['MA'] = stock_data['Close'].rolling(window=days).mean()
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"An error occurred while processing the data: {e}")
        return

    print(stock_data[['Date', 'MA']])

def main():
    """
    Main function to prompt user input and calculate the moving average.
    """
    file_name = input("Enter the stock data CSV file name: ")
    try:
        days = int(input("Enter the number of days for the moving average: "))
        if days < 1:
            raise ValueError("Number of days must be at least 1.")
        calculate_moving_average(file_name, days)
    except ValueError as e:
        print(f"Invalid input: {e}")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

<#sep#> need to take a loss trading out or lower the funds NAV. Vanguard's fund, being diversified in both term and in the traditional sense, would experience a rise in coupon payments from the short term bonds with higher yields, as well as it still receives a coupon on a lower priced bond that it now might just hold onto and not claim the loss and accept a lower yield and lower NAV. This is where the lower prices come from. But a loss can be good too depending on the circumstances. Also some bonds on the books are expiring naturally anyway, and would then just be bought at the higher yield/NAV.

Therefore, the bond fund NAV from the newer bonds on the books, plus reinvested fund distributions to you, could negate some of that bear action. 🐻

Oh what generosity the king has bestowed upon us.<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing an `InventoryManager` class for a retail store's inventory system. The class should support the following operations:

1. **Add Item**: Add a new item to the inventory or update the quantity of an existing item.
2. **Update Quantity**: Update the quantity of an existing item. If the item does not exist, raise a `KeyError`.
3. **Remove Item**: Remove an item from the inventory. If the item does not exist, raise a `KeyError`.
4. **List Items**: Return a dictionary of all items in the inventory with their quantities.
5. **Total Items**: Return the total number of items in the inventory.
6. **Find Item**: Return the quantity of a specified item. If the item does not exist, return 0.

The `InventoryManager` class should use a dictionary to store the items, with the item name as the key and the quantity as the value.

**Sample Input/Output:**

```
inventory = InventoryManager()
inventory.add_item("apple", 30)
inventory.add_item("banana", 45)
inventory.update_quantity("apple", 20)
print(inventory.list_items())  # Output: {'apple': 20, 'banana': 45}
print(inventory.total_items())  # Output: 65
print(inventory.find_item("banana"))  # Output: 45
inventory.remove_item("apple")
print(inventory.list_items())  # Output: {'banana': 45}
```

```python
class InventoryManager:
    def __init__(self):
        """Initialize an empty dictionary to store inventory items."""
        self.items = {}

    def add_item(self, item_name, quantity):
        """Add or update the quantity of the item in the inventory."""
        if item_name in self.items:
            self.items[item_name] += quantity
        else:
            self.items[item_name] = quantity

    def update_quantity(self, item_name, quantity):
        """Update the quantity of an existing item. Raise KeyError if item does not exist."""
        if item_name in self.items:
            self.items[item_name] = quantity
        else:
            raise KeyError(f"Item '{item_name}' not found in inventory.")

    def remove_item(self, item_name):
        """Remove an item from the inventory. Raise KeyError if item does not exist."""
        if item_name in self.items:
            del self.items[item_name]
        else:
            raise KeyError(f"Item '{item_name}' not found in inventory.")

    def list_items(self):
        """Return a dictionary of all items in the inventory with their quantities."""
        return self.items

    def total_items(self):
        """Return the total number of items in the inventory."""
        return sum(self.items.values())

    def find_item(self, item_name):
        """Return the quantity of a specified item. Return 0 if item does not exist."""
        return self.items.get(item_name, 0)

Hi @Colin-b thanks for this library - it helps people implementing OAuth flows which is great!
I think we might have discovered an issue that causes unintended or unexpected token reuse across requests/auth objects. 

The good news is we found an option that prevents it with the current version (see Considered options to mitigate the issue) but that might not be reasonable defaults (at least for the `OAuth2ClientCredentials`) or not very obvious for users.

Let me know what you think and what you think about potential solutions to the problem.

## Problem
When using the `auth` parameter (for example with `OAuth2ClientCredentials`) to authenticate with a service, the global token cache is shared across all `auth` objects for the same token urls. This can lead to unintended token reuse across different `auth` objects, for example, when using Microsoft Entra Id (i.e., Azure AD) to authenticate with different services via OAuth2.

This could cause security issues because (not exhaustive):

* An application may use different credentials to segregate access to different resources (for example dfor different users, different API calls, and so on). The global token cache may lead to reusing token across different `auth` objects, leading to unintended access to resources.

* Updating a secret during runtime may not invalidate the token cache, delaying the effect of the secret update.

Above behaviour might not be expected by the user and could lead to security issues.

## Sample to reproduce

I have written a few pytest test cases to demonstrate the issue.

```python
# token_reuse_example.py

"""Example of unexpected token reuse in httpx_auth.

How to reproduce:
1. Install the required dependencies:
    $ pip install httpx pytest httpx_auth
2. Create a .env file with the following content:
    # Use a OAUTH protected endpoint or webhook.io to check what Bearer is used.
    OAUTH_PROTECTED_ENDPOINT="https://graph.microsoft.com/v1.0/servicePrincipals/{REPLACE_WITH_OBJECT_ID_OF_ENTERPRISE_APP}" (or any other OAuth2 protected endpoint)
    CLIENT_SECRET="XXXXXXXX"
    CLIENT_ID="XXXXXXXX"
    # Replace with a tenant id
    TOKEN_URL="https://login.microsoftonline.com/XXXXXXXX-XXXX-XXXX-XXXX-XXX#XXXXXXXXX/oauth2/v2.0/token"
    # Optional: Add a second client_id and client_secret to test with multiple clients
    CLIENT_2_ID="<CLIENT_2_ID>"
    CLIENT_2_SECRET="<CLIENT_2_SECRET>"
3. Run the following commands:
    $ source .env && export $(cat .env | xargs)

4. Run the test:
    $ pytest token_reuse_example.py
"""

import os
from hashlib import sha512

import httpx
import pytest
from httpx_auth import OAuth2ClientCredentials
from httpx_auth._errors import InvalidGrantRequest
from httpx_auth._oauth2.common import OAuth2

OAUTH_PROTECTED_EP = os.getenv("OAUTH_PROTECTED_ENDPOINT")
CLIENT_SECRET = os.getenv("CLIENT_SECRET")
CLIENT_ID = os.getenv("CLIENT_ID")
CLIENT_2_ID = os.getenv("CLIENT_2_ID", "<CLIENT_2_ID>")
CLIENT_2_SECRET = os.getenv("CLIENT_2_SECRET", "<CLIENT_2_SECRET>")
TOKEN_URL = os.getenv("TOKEN_URL")


def test_current_behaviour():
    """Test to demonstrate current behaviour.

    The token is reused between requests and auth objects
    even if client_id and client_secret are different.
    """

    OAuth2.token_cache.clear()  # Ensure the cache is empty (due to the different test cases)

    with httpx.Client() as client:
        auth_c1 = OAuth2ClientCredentials(
            TOKEN_URL,
            client_id=CLIENT_ID,
            client_secret=CLIENT_SECRET,
            scope=".default",
        )

        response = client.get(OAUTH_PROTECTED_EP, auth=auth_c1)
        assert response.status_code == 200

    with httpx.Client() as client:
        auth_c1 = OAuth2ClientCredentials(
            TOKEN_URL,
            client_id="XXXXXXX",
            client_secret=CLIENT_SECRET,
            scope=".default",
        )

        response = client.get(OAUTH_PROTECTED_EP, auth=auth_c1)
        assert response.status_code == 200

    with httpx.Client() as client:
        auth_c1 = OAuth2ClientCredentials(
            TOKEN_URL, client_id=CLIENT_ID, client_secret="XXXXXXXXXX", scope=".default"
        )

        response = client.get(OAUTH_PROTECTED_EP, auth=auth_c1)
        assert response.status_code == 200


def test_current_behaviour_multiple_clients():
    """Test to demonstrate current behaviour with multiple clients.

    If the same TOKEN_URL is used, the token is reused between requests and auth objects.

    If CLIENT_2_ID and CLIENT_2_SECRET are provided, the test will use a different client_id and client_secret.
    Yet, the token fetched with `auth_c1` is reused between requests and auth objects.

    If CLIENT_2_ID and CLIENT_2_SECRET are not provided, the test will use default values that are invalid.
    Yet, the token from the first request is reused for the second request.
    """
    OAuth2.token_cache.clear()  # Ensure the cache is empty (due to the different test cases)

    with httpx.Client() as client_1, httpx.Client() as client_2:
        auth_c1 = OAuth2ClientCredentials(
            TOKEN_URL,
            client_id=CLIENT_ID,
            client_secret=CLIENT_SECRET,
            scope=".default",
        )
        auth_c2 = OAuth2ClientCredentials(
            TOKEN_URL,
            client_id=CLIENT_2_ID,
            client_secret=CLIENT_2_SECRET,
            scope=".default",
        )

        response = client_1.get(OAUTH_PROTECTED_EP, auth=auth_c1)
        assert response.status_code == 200

        response = client_2.get(OAUTH_PROTECTED_EP, auth=auth_c2)
        assert response.status_code == 200


def test_avoid_shared_cache_for_current_behaviour():
    """Test to demonstrate a workaround for the current behaviour.

    As the TokenCache uses the `self.state` instance variable to compute the cache key a
    current workaround is to use pass an additional kwarg parameter to the OAuth2ClientCredentials.

    We build a hash from the client_id and client_secret and pass it as as the `_client_id_secret_hash`
    argument to enforce the cache key to be different for different client_id and client_secret combinations.
    """

    OAuth2.token_cache.clear()  # Ensure the cache is empty (due to the different test cases)

    with httpx.Client() as client:
        _client_id_secret_hash = build_client_id_secret_hash(CLIENT_ID, CLIENT_SECRET)
        auth = OAuth2ClientCredentials(
            TOKEN_URL,
            client_id=CLIENT_ID,
            client_secret=CLIENT_SECRET,
            scope=".default",
            _client_id_secret_hash=_client_id_secret_hash,
        )

        response = client.get(OAUTH_PROTECTED_EP, auth=auth)
        assert response.status_code == 200

    with httpx.Client() as client:
        _client_id_secret_hash = build_client_id_secret_hash(CLIENT_ID, "XXXXXXXXXX")
        auth = OAuth2ClientCredentials(
            TOKEN_URL,
            client_id=CLIENT_ID,
            client_secret="XXXXXXXXXX",
            scope=".default",
            _client_id_secret_hash=_client_id_secret_hash,
        )

        with pytest.raises(InvalidGrantRequest):
            client.get(OAUTH_PROTECTED_EP, auth=auth)

    with httpx.Client() as client:
        _client_id_secret_hash = build_client_id_secret_hash("XXXXXXX", CLIENT_SECRET)
        auth = OAuth2ClientCredentials(
            TOKEN_URL,
            client_id="XXXXXXX",
            client_secret=CLIENT_SECRET,
            scope=".default",
            _client_id_secret_hash=_client_id_secret_hash,
        )

        with pytest.raises(InvalidGrantRequest):
            client.get(OAUTH_PROTECTED_EP, auth=auth)


def build_client_id_secret_hash(client_id: str, client_secret: str) -> str:
    return sha512(f"{client_id}{client_secret}".encode("unicode_escape")).hexdigest()

```

## Considered options to mitigate the issue

I propose the following options to mitigate the issue. Please note that the following might not be exhaustive and there might be other ways to mitigate the issue.

1. Warn the user about the global token cache and the cache key implementation and the potential issues it might cause more explicitly in the documentation and the code (e.g., in the docstring of the `OAuth2ClientCredentials` class). Point them to passing a hash like we did in the sample with `build_client_id_secret_hash`. 


2. Tie the token cache to the `auth` object. This would mean that the token cache is not shared across different `auth` objects.

    ```python
    class OAuth2BaseAuth(abc.ABC, httpx.Auth):
        # ...
        
        def auth_flow(
            self, request: httpx<#sep#># Optimization Lecture 1 Review of Differential Calculus for Functions of Single Variable.

12 Eponentil & Logrithm Functions The Euler s number e is pproimtely equl to.7188 nd is represented by the infinite sum e = f () = e The eponentil function is defined by The logrithm function<#sep#>Implement a Binary Search Tree (BST) in Python. Your BST should support the following operations:
- Insert a new key.
- Search for a key.
- Delete a key.

The input will be a series of operations, each on a new line. The first character of each line will be 'I' for insert, 'S' for search, and 'D' for delete, followed by a space and an integer key. For each search operation, output "Found" if the key is present in the BST, otherwise output "Not found".

**Sample Input:**
```
I 50
I 30
I 20
I 40
I 70
I 60
I 80
S 60
S 100
D 20
D 30
D 50
S 20
S 30
S 50
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Found
Not found
Not found
Not found
Not found
```

```python
class TreeNode:
    def __init__(self, key):
        self.left = None
        self.right = None
        self.val = key

class BinarySearchTree:
    def __init__(self):
        self.root = None

    def insert(self, key):
        """Insert a new key into the BST."""
        if self.root is None:
            self.root = TreeNode(key)
        else:
            self._insert(self.root, key)

    def _insert(self, node, key):
        if key < node.val:
            if node.left is None:
                node.left = TreeNode(key)
            else:
                self._insert(node.left, key)
        else:
            if node.right is None:
                node.right = TreeNode(key)
            else:
                self._insert(node.right, key)

    def search(self, key):
        """Search for a key in the BST. Return the node if found, otherwise None."""
        return self._search(self.root, key)

    def _search(self, node, key):
        if node is None or node.val == key:
            return node
        if key < node.val:
            return self._search(node.left, key)
        return self._search(node.right, key)

    def delete(self, key):
        """Delete a key from the BST."""
        self.root = self._delete(self.root, key)

    def _delete(self, node, key):
        if node is None:
            return node

        if key < node.val:
            node.left = self._delete(node.left, key)
        elif key > node.val:
            node.right = self._delete(node.right, key)
        else:
            if node.left is None:
                return node.right
            elif node.right is None:
                return node.left

            node.val = self._min_value_node(node.right).val
            node.right = self._delete(node.right, node.val)
        
        return node

    def _min_value_node(self, node):
        current = node
        while current.left is not None:
            current = current.left
        return current

# Example usage:
bst = BinarySearchTree()
operations = [
    "I 50", "I 30", "I 20", "I 40", "I 70", "I 60", "I 80",
    "S 60", "S 100", "D 20", "D 30", "D 50", "S 20", "S 30", "S 50"
]

for operation in operations:
    op, key = operation.split()
    key = int(key)
    if op == 'I':
        bst.insert(key)
    elif op == 'S':
        print("Found" if bst.search(key) else "Not found")
    elif op == 'D':
        bst.delete(key)

. E<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `BankAccount` class in Python. The class should support the following operations:

- **Initialization**: Create a bank account with an account number, account holder's name, and an optional initial balance (default is 0).
- **Deposit**: Add a specified amount to the account balance. The amount must be positive; otherwise, raise a `ValueError`.
- **Withdraw**: Subtract a specified amount from the account balance. The amount must be positive and should not exceed the current balance; otherwise, raise a `ValueError`.
- **Get Balance**: Return the current balance of the account.

**Sample Input/Output**:
```python
account = BankAccount("123456789", "John Doe", 100)
account.deposit(50)
print(account.get_balance())  # Output: 150
account.withdraw(30)
print(account.get_balance())  # Output: 120
account.withdraw(150)         # Raises ValueError: Insufficient funds.
account.deposit(-10)          # Raises ValueError: Deposit amount must be positive.
```

```python
class BankAccount:
    def __init__(self, account_number, account_holder_name, balance=0):
        """
        Initialize a new bank account.

        :param account_number: A string representing the account number.
        :param account_holder_name: A string representing the account holder's name.
        :param balance: An optional float representing the initial balance (default is 0).
        """
        self.account_number = account_number
        self.account_holder_name = account_holder_name
        self.balance = balance

    def deposit(self, amount):
        """
        Deposit a specified amount into the account.

        :param amount: A float representing the amount to deposit.
        :raises ValueError: If the amount is not positive.
        """
        if amount > 0:
            self.balance += amount
        else:
            raise ValueError("Deposit amount must be positive.")

    def withdraw(self, amount):
        """
        Withdraw a specified amount from the account.

        :param amount: A float representing the amount to withdraw.
        :raises ValueError: If the amount is not positive or exceeds the current balance.
        """
        if amount > self.balance:
            raise ValueError("Insufficient funds.")
        elif amount <= 0:
            raise ValueError("Withdrawal amount must be positive.")
        else:
            self.balance -= amount

    def get_balance(self):
        """
        Get the current balance of the account.

        :return: A float representing the current balance.
        """
        return self.balance
