</think>To compute \(\det(\mathbf{A}^2 - 2 \mathbf{A})\) where \(\mathbf{A} = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 3 \\ 2 & 1 \end{pmatrix}\), we proceed as follows:

1. **Compute \(\mathbf{A}^2\):**
   \[
   \mathbf{A}^2 = \mathbf{A} \cdot \mathbf{A} = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 3 \\ 2 & 1 \end{pmatrix} \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 3 \\ 2 & 1 \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} 1 \cdot 1 + 3 \cdot 2 & 1 \cdot 3 + 3 \cdot 1 \\ 2 \cdot 1 + 1 \cdot 2 & 2 \cdot 3 + 1 \cdot 1 \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} 7 & 6 \\ 4 & 7 \end{pmatrix}
   \]

2. **Compute \(2 \mathbf{A}\):**
   \[
   2 \mathbf{A} = 2 \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 3 \\ 2 & 1 \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 6 \\ 4 & 2 \end{pmatrix}
   \]

3. **Subtract \(2 \mathbf{A}\) from \(\mathbf{A}^2\):**
   \[
   \mathbf{A}^2 - 2 \mathbf{A} = \begin{pmatrix} 7 & 6 \\ 4 & 7 \end{pmatrix} - \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 6 \\ 4 & 2 \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} 5 & 0 \\ 0 & 5 \end{pmatrix}
   \]

4. **Compute the determinant of the resulting matrix:**
   \[
   \det\left(\begin{pmatrix} 5 & 0 \\ 0 & 5 \end{pmatrix}\right) = 5 \cdot 5 - 0 \cdot 0 = 25
   \]

Therefore, the determinant \(\det(\mathbf{A}^2 - 2 \mathbf{A})\) is:
\[
\boxed{25}
\]

Alternatively, we can use the property of determinants:
1. **Factor \(\mathbf{A}^2 - 2 \mathbf{A}\) as \(\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{A} - 2\mathbf{I})\):**
   \[
   \det(\mathbf{A}^2 - 2 \mathbf{A}) = \det(\mathbf{A}) \cdot \det(\mathbf{A} - 2\mathbf{I})
   \]

2. **Compute \(\det(\mathbf{A})\):**
   \[
   \det(\mathbf{A}) = 1 \cdot 1 - 3 \cdot 2 = -5
   \]

3. **Compute \(\mathbf{A} - 2\mathbf{I}\) and its determinant:**
   \[
   \mathbf{A} - 2\mathbf{I} = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 3 \\ 2 & 1 \end{pmatrix} - \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 0 \\ 0 & 2 \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} -1 & 3 \\ 2 & -1 \end{pmatrix}
   \]
   \[
   \det(\mathbf{A} - 2\mathbf{I}) = (-1) \cdot (-1) - 3 \cdot 2 = 1 - 6 = -5
   \]

4. **Multiply the determinants:**
   \[
   \det(\mathbf{A}) \cdot \det(\mathbf{A} - 2\mathbf{I}) = (-5) \cdot (-5) = 25
   \]

Both methods confirm that the determinant is:
\[
\boxed{25}

### Loudoun Valley High School Calculus Summertime Fun Packet

<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `Book` class for a library management system. The class should support the following functionalities:

- **Attributes:**
  - `title`: A string representing the title of the book.
  - `author`: A string representing the author of the book.
  - `isbn`: A string representing the ISBN of the book.
  - `copies_available`: An integer representing the number of copies currently available in the library.

- **Methods:**
  - `__init__(self, title, author, isbn, copies_available)`: Initializes the book with the given title, author, ISBN, and number of copies available.
  - `borrow_book(self)`: Decreases the number of copies available by 1 if there are any copies left. If no copies are available, it should print "No copies available to borrow."
  - `return_book(self)`: Increases the number of copies available by 1.
  - `get_details(self)`: Returns a formatted string containing the book's title, author, and ISBN.

**Sample Input/Output:**
```python
book = Book("1984", "George Orwell", "9780451524935", 3)
print(book.get_details())  # Output: Title: 1984, Author: George Orwell, ISBN: 9780451524935
book.borrow_book()
print(book.copies_available)  # Output: 2
book.return_book()
print(book.copies_available)  # Output: 3
book.borrow_book()
book.borrow_book()
book.borrow_book()
book.borrow_book()  # Output: No copies available to borrow.
```

```python
class Book:
    def __init__(self, title, author, isbn, copies_available):
        """
        Initializes a new book with the given title, author, ISBN, and number of copies available.
        
        :param title: str - The title of the book.
        :param author: str - The author of the book.
        :param isbn: str - The ISBN of the book.
        :param copies_available: int - The number of copies available in the library.
        """
        self.title = title
        self.author = author
        self.isbn = isbn
        self.copies_available = copies_available

    def borrow_book(self):
        """
        Decreases the number of copies available by 1 if there are any copies left.
        If no copies are available, it prints "No copies available to borrow."
        """
        if self.copies_available > 0:
            self.copies_available -= 1
        else:
            print("No copies available to borrow.")

    def return_book(self):
        """
        Increases the number of copies available by 1.
        """
        self.copies_available += 1

    def get_details(self):
        """
        Returns a formatted string containing the book's title, author, and ISBN.
        
        :return: str - The formatted string with book details.
        """
        return f"Title: {self.title}, Author: {self.author}, ISBN: {self.isbn}"
```<#sep#>Given $4 \times 4$ matrices $A$ and $B$ with eigenvalues $x_1, x_2, x_3, x_4$ for $A$ and $\frac{1}{x_1}, \frac{1}{x_2}, \frac{1}{x_3}, \frac{1}{x_4}$ for $B$, where $x_1, x_2, x_3, x_4$ are real numbers greater than 1, provide an example of $A$ and $B$ such that $1$ is not an eigenvalue of $AB$.

Mth 360: A primitive integrl nd elementry functions D. DeTurck University of<#sep#>You are tasked with designing a Python class `Employee` to represent an employee in a company. The class should have the following attributes: `name`, `position`, `salary`, and `department`. Implement the following methods:
- `give_raise(amount)`: Increases the employee's salary by the given amount.
- `transfer(new_department)`: Changes the employee's department to the new department.
- `__str__()`: Returns a string representation of the employee, including all attributes.

Additionally, design a second class `Manager` that inherits from `Employee` and adds an additional attribute `subordinates` which is a list of `Employee` objects. The `Manager` class should also have a method `add_subordinate(employee)` that adds an `Employee` object to the list of subordinates.

**Sample Input:**
```
emp1 = Employee("John Doe", "Developer", 70000, "Engineering")
mg1 = Manager("Jane Smith", "Director", 150000, "Engineering")
mg1.add_subordinate(emp1)
emp1.give_raise(5000)
emp1.transfer("Product")
mg1.add_subordinate(Employee("Alice Johnson", "Designer", 80000, "Product"))
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Employee(Name: John Doe, Position: Developer, Salary: 75000, Department: Product)
Manager(Name: Jane Smith, Position: Director, Salary: 150000, Department: Engineering, Subordinates: [Employee(Name: John Doe, Position: Developer, Salary: 75000, Department: Product), Employee(Name: Alice Johnson, Position: Designer, Salary: 80000, Department: Product)])
```

```python
class Employee:
    def __init__(self, name, position, salary, department):
        """
        Initializes an Employee object with the given attributes.
        
        :param name: The name of the employee.
        :param position: The position of the employee.
        :param salary: The salary of the employee.
        :param department: The department of the employee.
        """
        self.name = name
        self.position = position
        self.salary = salary
        self.department = department
    
    def give_raise(self, amount):
        """
        Increases the employee's salary by the given amount.
        
        :param amount: The amount to increase the salary by.
        """
        self.salary += amount

    def transfer(self, new_department):
        """
        Changes the employee's department to the new department.
        
        :param new_department: The new department for the employee.
        """
        self.department = new_department
    
    def __str__(self):
        """
        Returns a string representation of the employee.
        
        :return: A string representation of the employee.
        """
        return f"Employee(Name: {self.name}, Position: {self.position}, Salary: {self.salary}, Department: {self.department})"


class Manager(Employee):
    def __init__(self, name, position, salary, department):
        """
        Initializes a Manager object with the given attributes and an empty list of subordinates.
        
        :param name: The name of the manager.
        :param position: The position of the manager.
        :param salary: The salary of the manager.
        :param department: The department of the manager.
        """
        super().__init__(name, position, salary, department)
        self.subordinates = []
    
    def add_subordinate(self, employee):
        """
        Adds an Employee object to the list of subordinates.
        
        :param employee: The Employee object to add as a subordinate.
        """
        self.subordinates.append(employee)
    
    def __str__(self):
        """
        Returns a string representation of the manager and their subordinates.
        
        :return: A string representation of the manager and their subordinates.
        """
        subordinates_str = ", ".join(str(sub) for sub in self.subordinates)
        return f"Manager(Name: {self.name}, Position: {self.position}, Salary: {self.salary}, Department: {self.department}, Subordinates: [{subordinates_str}])"

Let’s<#sep#>JSONDecodeError due to Improper Handling of Nested JSON Strings in JWT Payloads
## Description

There is an issue in the `httpx-auth` library where the decoding of base64-encoded JSON within JWT tokens corrupts JSON strings that contain nested JSON. This happens because the double quotes inside the nested JSON string are not correctly handled during the decoding process, leading to a failure when attempting to load the string back into a JSON object.

## Steps to Reproduce

The issue can be reproduced with the following test case:

```python
import jwt
import json
from httpx_auth._oauth2.tokens import decode_base64

def test_decode_base64_with_nested_json_string():
    # Encode a JSON inside the JWT
    dummy_token = jwt.encode({"data": json.dumps({"something": ["else"]})}, key="")
    header, body, signature = dummy_token.split(".")
    
    # Decode the body
    decoded_bytes = decode_base64(body)
    
    # Attempt to load JSON
    result = json.loads(decoded_bytes)
    assert result == {"data": '{"something": ["else"]}'}
```

Running this test results in a json.decoder.JSONDecodeError due to incorrect handling of the nested JSON string.

## Expected Behavior
The decoded JSON string should be handled correctly, allowing for proper loading into a Python dictionary without JSON parsing errors.

## Actual Behavior
The test raises the following error due to malformed JSON:
```shell
json.decoder.JSONDecodeError: Expecting ',' delimiter: line 1 column 12 (char 11)
```

This error is caused by the way double quotes inside the nested JSON are handled, which corrupts the JSON string during the base64 decoding step.

## Environment
Python Version: 3.10.11
httpx-auth version: 0.22.0 (2024-03-02)

## Additional Context
This issue impacts scenarios where JWT tokens contain nested JSON strings as part of their payload. A fix would likely involve adjusting the base64 decoding function to correctly handle nested JSON strings without corrupting them.

User Input:
Height
```

**Sample Output:**
```
An error occurred: The column 'Height' does not exist in the CSV file.
```

```python
import csv

def calculate_average_from_csv(file_path, column_name):
    """
    Reads the CSV file and calculates the average of the specified column.

    :param file_path: Path to the CSV file.
    :param column_name: The column name for which we want to calculate the average.
    :return: Average value of the column.
    """
    try:
        with open(file_path, mode='r', newline='', encoding='utf-8') as file:
            reader = csv.DictReader(file)
            total_sum = 0
            count = 0

            for row in reader:
                try:
                    value = float(row[column_name])
                    total_sum += value
                    count += 1
                except ValueError:
                    print(f"Warning: Non-numeric value '{row[column_name]}' in column '{column_name}' ignored.")
            
            if count == 0:
                raise ValueError(f"No numeric values found in column '{column_name}'.")
            
            average = total_sum / count
            return average

    except FileNotFoundError:
        raise FileNotFoundError(f"The file at path '{file_path}' was not found.")
    except KeyError:
        raise KeyError(f"The column '{column_name}' does not exist in the CSV file.")

if __name__ == '__main__':
    file_path = 'data.csv'
    print("CSV file to be used:", file_path)
    column_name = input("Enter the name of the column to calculate the average for: ")

    try:
        average_value = calculate_average_from_csv(file_path, column_name)
        print(f"The average value in column '{column_name}' is: {average_value:.2f}")
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"An error occurred: {e}")

### Chapter 4 Contravariance, Covariance, and Spacetime Diagrams

Chpter 4 Contrvrince, Covrince, nd Spcetime Digrms 4. The Components of Vector in Skewed Coordintes We hve seen in Chpter <#sep#>You are given a string `text` and an integer `n`. Your task is to implement a function `find_most_common_words` that returns a list of the `n` most common words in the string, excluding common English stop words and ignoring punctuation and case. The words should be returned as a list of tuples, where each tuple contains the word and its frequency.

**Constraints:**
- The string `text` will contain at least 1 word and at most 10,000 characters.
- The integer `n` will be at least 1 and at most the number of unique words in the text excluding stop words.

**Stop Words:**
Ignore the following common English stop words: 'the', 'and', 'a', 'an', 'in', 'at', 'on', 'of', 'to', 'for', 'with', 'by', 'as', 'that', 'from', 'it', 'is', 'was', 'were', 'but', 'or', 'if', 'then', 'than', 'too', 'very', 'can', 'will', 'just', 'don', 'should', 'now', 'he', 'she', 'they', 'them', 'his', 'her', 'their', 'its', 'be', 'been', 'being', 'have', 'has', 'had', 'do', 'does', 'did', 'so', 'up', 'out', 'about', 'into', 'over', 'after', 'under', 'again', 'further', 'once', 'here', 'there', 'when', 'where', 'why', 'how', 'all', 'any', 'both', 'each', 'few', 'more', 'most', 'other', 'some', 'such', 'no', 'nor', 'not', 'only', 'own', 'same', 'too', 'very', 's', 't', 'can', 'will', 'just', 'don', 'should', 'now'.

**Sample Input:**
```
text = "In the town of Athy one morning Early in May While the dew was still on the grass The小伙子 from Kildare That dreamed of apples orchards As he a'hunting and a'hounding And singing of his "Delilah" Chanced to meet Napper Tandy And Delilah of the wild harp Landley"
n = 3
```

**Sample Output:**
```
[('delilah', 2), ('and', 2), ('the', 2)]
```

```python
import re
from collections import Counter

def find_most_common_words(text, n):
    """
    Returns a list of the n most common words in the given text, excluding common English stop words,
    ignoring punctuation and case. Each word is returned as a tuple containing the word and its frequency.
    
    :param text: A string containing the text to analyze.
    :param n: An integer specifying the number of most common words to return.
    :return: A list of tuples, each containing a word and its frequency.
    """
    stop_words = set([
        'the', 'and', 'a', 'an', 'in', 'at', 'on', 'of', 'to', 'for', 'with', 'by', 'as', 'that', 'from', 'it', 'is', 'was', 'were',
        'but', 'or', 'if', 'then', 'than', 'too', 'very', 'can', 'will', 'just', 'don', 'should', 'now', 'he', 'she', 'they',
        'them', 'his', 'her', 'their', 'its', 'be', 'been', 'being', 'have', 'has', 'had', 'do', 'does', 'did', 'so', 'up',
        'out', 'about', 'into', 'over', 'after', 'under', 'again', 'further', 'once', 'here', 'there', 'when', 'where',
        'why', 'how', 'all', 'any', 'both', 'each', 'few', 'more', 'most', 'other', 'some', 'such', 'no', 'nor', 'not', 'only',
        'own', 'same', 'too', 'very', 's', 't', 'can', 'will', 'just', 'don', 'should', 'now'
    ])
    
    words = re.findall(r'\b\w+\b', text.lower())
    filtered_words = [word for word in words if word not in stop_words]
    word_counts = Counter(filtered_words)
    most_common_words = word_counts.most_common(n)
    
    return most_common_words
```<#sep#> B would be the diagonal matrix with entries 1/3, 1/4, 1/5, 1/2 (a cyclic permutation). Then AB would be the diagonal matrix with entries (2*(1/3)) = 2/3, (3*(1/4)) = 3/4, (4*(1/5)) = 4/5, and (5*(1/2)) = 5/2. The eigenvalues of AB are 2/3, 3/4, 4/5, and 5/2. None of these are 1, so 1 is not an eigenvalue of AB. 

Therefore, this satisfies the conditions. So, in this case, A is a diagonal matrix, B is a diagonal matrix with the reciprocals permuted cyclically, and their product AB has eigenvalues not including 1. 

But wait, the problem states that B has eigenvalues 1/x1, 1/x2, 1/x3, 1/x4. If we permute the reciprocals, as in the example above, then B's eigenvalues are still the same set {1/x1, 1/x2, 1/x3, 1/x4}, just ordered differently. Since eigenvalues are a set, their order doesn't matter. Therefore, this is acceptable. 

Therefore, such matrices A and B exist, and an example is when A is diagonal with distinct entries greater than 1, and B is a diagonal matrix with the reciprocals permuted such that no reciprocal is in the position corresponding to its original eigenvalue. 

Alternatively, if we don't restrict to diagonal matrices, we can use permutation matrices. Suppose A is diagonal, and B is a permutation matrix times the diagonal matrix of reciprocals. But in that case, B might not be diagonal, and its eigenvalues would be products of the reciprocals and the permutation's eigenvalues, which could be complex. However, the problem states that B has eigenvalues 1/x1, ..., 1/x4, which are all real. Therefore, B must be similar to a diagonal matrix with those entries, so if we use a permutation matrix to permute the reciprocals, since permutation matrices are orthogonal, B would be similar to the diagonal matrix, hence having real eigenvalues. Wait, but permutation matrices can have eigenvalues that are complex if the permutation is not a product of disjoint transpositions (in even dimensions). For example, a 4x4 permutation matrix corresponding to a 4-cycle has eigenvalues that are 4th roots of unity, which include ±1, ±i. Therefore, B would have eigenvalues which are not all real, which contradicts the problem's statement that B's eigenvalues are 1/xi, which are real. 

Hence, this approach might not work. Therefore, perhaps the safest way is to take B as a diagonal matrix with the reciprocals permuted. Since diagonal matrices with permuted entries are similar via permutation matrices, which are orthogonal, hence B is diagonalizable with real eigenvalues (the reciprocals), so it's acceptable. 

Therefore, to answer the problem, we can take A as a diagonal matrix with distinct entries greater than 1, and B as a diagonal matrix with the reciprocals permuted in a derangement (no element in its original position), ensuring that AB's diagonal entries (which are the products) don't include 1, hence 1 is not an eigenvalue. 

Therefore, an explicit example is:

A = diag(2, 3, 4, 5)

B = diag(1/3, 1/4, 1/5, 1/2)

Then AB = diag(2/3, 3/4, 4/5, 5/2), which has eigenvalues 2/3, 3/4, 4/5, 5/2. None of these are 1, so 1 is not an eigenvalue of AB. 

Alternatively, since the problem allows for any such matrices, not necessarily diagonal, but this example suffices. Therefore, this should be a valid solution.
</think>To construct matrices \( A \) and \( B \) such that \( 1 \) is not an eigenvalue of \( AB \), we can use diagonal matrices where the eigenvalues of \( A \) are distinct real numbers greater than 1, and the eigenvalues of \( B \) are the reciprocals of those of \( A \) but permuted in such a way that no eigenvalue of \( A \) multiplies its own reciprocal in the product \( AB \).

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Define Matrix \( A \):**
   Let \( A \) be a diagonal matrix with distinct eigenvalues greater than 1. For example:
   \[
   A = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 0 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 3 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 4 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & 5 \end{pmatrix}
   \]
   The eigenvalues of \( A \) are \( 2, 3, 4, 5 \).

2. **Define Matrix \( B \):**
   Let \( B \) be a diagonal matrix with the reciprocals of the eigenvalues of \( A \) permuted such that no eigenvalue of \( A \) multiplies its own reciprocal. For example, we can use the following permutation:
   \[
   B = \begin{pmatrix} \frac{1}{3} & 0 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & \frac{1}{4} & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & \frac{1}{5} & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & \frac{1}{2} \end{pmatrix}
   \]
   The eigenvalues of \( B \) are \( \frac{1}{3}, \frac{1}{4}, \frac{1}{5}, \frac{1}{2} \), which are the reciprocals of the eigenvalues of \( A \) permuted.

3. **Compute the Product \( AB \):**
   The product \( AB \) is:
   \[
   AB = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 0 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 3 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 4 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & 5 \end{pmatrix} \begin{pmatrix} \frac{1}{3} & 0 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & \frac{1}{4} & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & \frac{1}{5} & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & \frac{1}{2} \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} \frac{2}{3} & 0 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & \frac{3}{4} & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & \frac{4}{5} & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & \frac{5}{2} \end{pmatrix}
   \]
   The eigenvalues of \( AB \) are \( \frac{2}{3}, \frac{3}{4}, \frac{4}{5}, \frac{5}{2} \).

4. **Verify the Eigenvalues:**
   Since \( \frac{2}{3}, \frac{3}{4}, \frac{4}{5}, \frac{5}{2} \) are all distinct and none of them equal 1, \( 1 \) is not an eigenvalue of \( AB \).

Thus, the matrices \( A \) and \( B \) that satisfy the given conditions are:
\[
\boxed{A = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 0 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 3 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 4 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & 5 \end{pmatrix}, \quad B = \begin{pmatrix} \frac{1}{3} & 0 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & \frac{1}{4} & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & \frac{1}{5} & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & \frac{1}{2} \end{pmatrix}}

Suppose I choose any number of terms except one to be 1. Wait but even then, if after doing that, the product is less than desired, we can further split one of the 1-term into more terms. 

Each time you split a term into two smaller terms x and y (such that x+y = original term's value), the contribution to the product becomes (x+1)(y+1) if the old term was (r), which had (r+1) contribution. 

So splitting r into x and y, we have:

Old contribution: (r +1)

New contribution: (x+1)(y+1) → this is equal to (x+1)(y+1)= rx + ry +x + y +1 = original needed to be (r+1) plus some quantity. 

Wait, rx?

Wait the variables are such that x+y = r. Since the new terms' sum must be same as before. 

Thus, 

x + y = r → hence the sum remains same, so the product becomes (x+1)(y+1) = xy + x + y + 1 = (xy) + (r) +1 → and since (r+1) was the original contribution. 

Thus, splitting gives a new contribution of (xy + (r + 1)). 

Thus, the increase in the product is exactly xy, which has to be positive because x and y are positive.

Thus, every time you split a term into two, the product increases. 

Conversely, combining two terms (reverse process) decreases the product. 

Thus, we can get the desired product by starting with a minimal product and splitting terms until the product reaches b. 

The minimal product for a given a is achieved when all terms are as large as possible. The minimal product occurs when all but one are zero (แต่ because zero is not allowed), so the minimal is one term of a, with product (a+1). 当 the desired product is greater than this 最小值, we need to proceed splitting.

Therefore, if b > a+1, then we must split the initial single-term into multiple terms.

The strategy could be:

Start with one term of a. The product is (a+1).

While product <b:

Choose a term, split it into two terms in such a way that the product increases toward b.

Repeat until product reaches exactly b.

The question is how to split to reach exactly b.

This seems possible theoretically , but computationally, to represent this as a sequence.

But how to do this in code?

Since splitting increases the product, perhaps we can do this step-wise until we reach the desired product.

The current total product after replacement is this, so total product becomes: old / (2+1) * new = 3/3 * (x+1)(3 -x).

So can't reach 5 with only one split. So need to split further.

Ciao, I'd like to share a thought w/ you. Given the problem domain I think stripping the leading `- ` in category entries could be the right thing to do, you already assert an entry is an entry by placing it in relevant section after all.

That could be a future-proof evolution of the lib (eg. tomorrow KAC choose to use `*` instead of `-`) that could even keep it as a valuable resource should someone decide to implement a transcoding app.

And btw, I'm totally in a total [rust reimplementation](https://github.com/aogier/keep-a-changelog), getting faster speed by two order of magnitude. Do you like rust? I do like it, I'm still learning but so far so good!

ciao

More information<#sep#>Implement a simple singly linked list in Python. Your linked list should support the following operations:
- `insert(data)`: Inserts a new node with the given data at the end of the list.
- `delete(data)`: Deletes the first node with the given data from the list.
- `search(data)`: Returns `True` if a node with the given data exists in the list, otherwise returns `False`.
- `display()`: Prints the data of all nodes in the list in order.

**Sample Input:**
```
insert(10)
insert(20)
insert(30)
display()
search(20)
delete(20)
display()
search(20)
```

**Sample Output:**
```
10 20 30
True
10 30
False
```

```python
class Node:
    def __init__(self, data):
        """
        Initializes a new node with the given data and a pointer to the next node set to None.
        
        :param data: The data to be stored in the node.
        """
        self.data = data
        self.next = None

class LinkedList:
    def __init__(self):
        """
        Initializes an empty linked list with the head set to None.
        """
        self.head = None

    def insert(self, data):
        """
        Inserts a new node with the given data at the end of the list.
        
        :param data: The data to be inserted into the list.
        """
        new_node = Node(data)
        if not self.head:
            self.head = new_node
            return
        last = self.head
        while last.next:
            last = last.next
        last.next = new_node

    def delete(self, data):
        """
        Deletes the first node with the given data from the list.
        
        :param data: The data of the node to be deleted.
        """
        current = self.head
        previous = None
        while current and current.data != data:
            previous = current
            current = current.next
        if not current:
            return
        if not previous:
            self.head = current.next
        else:
            previous.next = current.next

    def search(self, data):
        """
        Returns True if a node with the given data exists in the list, otherwise returns False.
        
        :param data: The data to be searched in the list.
        :return: Boolean indicating the presence of the data in the list.
        """
        current = self.head
        while current:
            if current.data == data:
                return True
            current = current.next
        return False

    def display(self):
        """
        Prints the data of all nodes in the list in order.
        """
        current = self.head
        while current:
            print(current.data, end=" ")
            current = current.next
        print()

Hence, the five fractions equivalent to $\frac{5<#sep#>You are given a dataset of movie reviews stored in two files: `positive_reviews.txt` and `negative_reviews.txt`. Each file contains one review per line. Your task is to implement a function `classify_reviews` that takes a list of movie reviews as input and returns a list of predicted labels indicating whether each review is positive or negative. Use the Natural Language Toolkit (NLTK) and the Naive Bayes classifier for this task. Assume that the NLTK data files are already downloaded.

**Sample Input:**
- `positive_reviews.txt`:
  ```
  What an amazing movie!
  I loved the plot and acting.
  ```
- `negative_reviews.txt`:
  ```
  The movie was terrible.
  I did not enjoy the film at all.
  ```
- `new_reviews`:
  ```
  ["This movie was really awful. I hated it.",
   "A fantastic film with a great storyline and stunning performances."]
  ```

**Sample Output:**
```
['negative', 'positive']
```

```python
import nltk
from nltk.corpus import stopwords
from nltk.classify import NaiveBayesClassifier
from nltk.tokenize import word_tokenize
import string
from collections import Counter

# Ensure you have the NLTK data files downloaded
nltk.download('punkt')
nltk.download('stopwords')

def load_reviews(file_path):
    """
    Load reviews from a file.
    
    :param file_path: Path to the file containing reviews.
    :return: List of reviews.
    """
    with open(file_path, 'r', encoding='utf-8') as f:
        reviews = f.readlines()
    return reviews

def preprocess_text(text):
    """
    Preprocess the text by tokenizing, removing stopwords, punctuation, and converting to lower case.
    
    :param text: The text to preprocess.
    :return: List of preprocessed tokens.
    """
    stop_words = set(stopwords.words('english'))
    tokens = word_tokenize(text)
    tokens = [word.lower() for word in tokens if word.isalpha() and word not in stop_words]
    return tokens

def get_features(tokens):
    """
    Extract features from the tokens by counting the frequency of each word.
    
    :param tokens: List of tokens.
    :return: Dictionary of features.
    """
    token_counts = Counter(tokens)
    return {word: True for word in token_counts}

def train_classifier(positive_reviews, negative_reviews):
    """
    Train a Naive Bayes classifier using the provided positive and negative reviews.
    
    :param positive_reviews: List of positive reviews.
    :param negative_reviews: List of negative reviews.
    :return: Trained Naive Bayes classifier.
    """
    positive_features = [(get_features(preprocess_text(review)), 'positive') for review in positive_reviews]
    negative_features = [(get_features(preprocess_text(review)), 'negative') for review in negative_reviews]
    features = positive_features + negative_features
    classifier = NaiveBayesClassifier.train(features)
    return classifier

def classify_reviews(reviews, classifier):
    """
    Classify a list of reviews using the provided classifier.
    
    :param reviews: List of reviews to classify.
    :param classifier: Trained Naive Bayes classifier.
    :return: List of predicted labels.
    """
    return [classifier.classify(get_features(preprocess_text(review))) for review in reviews]

# Load reviews
positive_reviews = load_reviews('positive_reviews.txt')
negative_reviews = load_reviews('negative_reviews.txt')

# Train the classifier
classifier = train_classifier(positive_reviews, negative_reviews)

# Example usage
new_reviews = [
    "This movie was really awful. I hated it.",
    "A fantastic film with a great storyline and stunning performances."
]

predicted_labels = classify_reviews(new_reviews, classifier)
print(predicted_labels)
```<#sep#>where θ parameterizes the circle) will trace out a circle in the x-y-z coordinates. So for a given θ, the point on the generating circle is (3 + cos θ, 0, 1 + sin θ). Then, rotating this around the z-axis by angle φ, the coordinates become:

x = (3 + cos θ) cos φ

y = (3 + cos θ) sin φ

z = 1 + sin θ

So the parametric equations for the torus are:

x = (3 + cos θ) cos φ

y = (3 + cos θ) sin φ

z = 1 + sin θ

where θ and φ range from 0 to 2π.

Alternatively, this can be seen as a standard torus with major radius R = 3 and minor radius r = 1, but shifted up by 1 in the z-direction. Wait, no. The generating circle is at (3,0,1) with radius 1. So when you rotate around the z-axis, the major radius is 3, and the minor radius is 1. But the z-coordinate of the original circle is 1, so when rotated, the entire torus is shifted up by 1 in the z-direction? Wait, no. Because when you rotate the circle around the z-axis, the z-coordinate remains the same as the original circle. So the original circle is at z=1, and as we rotate it, all points on the torus will have z-coordinate varying between 0 and 2, since the original circle is centered at z=1 with radius 1. So the torus spans from z=0 to z=2. Wait, no. The original circle is centered at (3,0,1) with radius 1. The z-coordinate of the original circle is 1, and the radius in the x-z plane. So the original circle's equation is (x - 3)^2 + (z - 1)^2 = 1. Therefore, the z-coordinate of points on the original circle ranges from 0 to 2. Then, when you rotate this circle around the z-axis, each point on the circle will trace a circle in the x-y plane. But the z-coordinate remains the same. So the entire torus will have z-coordinates ranging from 0 to 2, same as the original circle. Therefore, the torus is located between z=0 and z=2.

Wait, but the center of the original circle is at (3,0,1). When you rotate this around the z-axis, the z-coordinate doesn't change, so the entire torus is at z=1, but with variation in z? Wait, no. Wait, the original circle is in the x-z plane. Wait, the circle is centered at (3,0,1), and has radius 1. So in the x-z plane (since y=0), the circle is (x-3)^2 + (z - 1)^2 = 1. So in x-z plane, this circle extends from x=2 to x=4 and z=0 to z=2. When rotated around the z-axis, which is the vertical axis, each point (x,0,z) on the circle will trace a circle in the x-y plane with radius x. So the torus is generated by all points (x, y, z) where (sqrt(x^2 + y^2) - 3)^2 + (z - 1)^2 = 1. That's the equation of the torus.

Therefore, the equation of the torus is (ρ - 3)^2 + (z - 1)^2 = 1, where ρ = sqrt(x^2 + y^2). So, in cylindrical coordinates, that's the equation.

Now, the sphere is centered at (0,0,h) with radius h (since it touches the plane z=0). We need to find the largest h such that the sphere touches the torus.

So, the distance from the center of the sphere (0,0,h) to any point on the torus must be at least h, and there must be at least one point where the distance is exactly h (the point of contact).

So, to find the minimal distance from (0,0,h) to the torus, and set that minimal distance equal to h.

Therefore, the problem reduces to finding the minimal distance between the point (0,0,h) and the torus, and setting that equal to h. Then solve for h.

But h is both the radius of the sphere and the z-coordinate of the center. So we have to find h such that the minimal distance from (0,0,h) to the torus is equal to h, which is the radius.

Alternatively, the sphere is tangent to the torus, so the closest point on the torus to (0,0,h) is at distance h from (0,0,h).

So let's compute the minimal distance between (0,0,h) and the torus.

Given the torus equation: (ρ - 3)^2 + (z - 1)^2 = 1, where ρ = sqrt(x^2 + y^2).

We need to find the minimal distance between (0,0,h) and a point (ρ cos φ, ρ sin φ, z) on the torus.

The distance squared is (ρ cos φ - 0)^2 + (ρ sin φ - 0)^2 + (z - h)^2 = ρ^2 + (z - h)^2.

But the point (ρ, z) must satisfy (ρ - 3)^2 + (z - 1)^2 = 1.

So the problem is to minimize ρ^2 + (z - h)^2 subject to (ρ - 3)^2 + (z - 1)^2 = 1.

This is a constrained optimization problem. We can use Lagrange multipliers to find the minimum.

Let’s denote f(ρ, z) = ρ^2 + (z - h)^2, the square of the distance.

The constraint is g(ρ, z) = (ρ - 3)^2 + (z - 1)^2 - 1 = 0.

Using Lagrange multipliers, the gradient of f must be proportional to the gradient of g.

Compute gradients:

∇f = (2ρ, 2(z - h))

∇g = (2(ρ - 3), 2(z - 1))

So, ∇f = λ ∇g:

2ρ = λ * 2(ρ - 3)

2(z - h) = λ * 2(z - 1)

Simplify:

ρ = λ(ρ - 3) → equation (1)

(z - h) = λ(z - 1) → equation (2)

And the constraint equation:

(ρ - 3)^2 + (z - 1)^2 = 1 → equation (3)

We need to solve these three equations for ρ, z, and λ.

From equation (1): ρ = λ(ρ - 3)

Solve for λ:

λ = ρ / (ρ - 3) → equation (1a)

From equation (2): z - h = λ(z - 1)

Substitute λ from (1a):

z - h = [ρ / (ρ - 3)] (z - 1) → equation (2a)

Now, we have two equations (equation 2a and equation 3) with variables ρ and z.

But this might get complicated. Alternatively, let's try to express z in terms of ρ or vice versa.

Alternatively, solve for z from equation (2a):

z - h = [ρ / (ρ - 3)] (z - 1)

Multiply both sides by (ρ - 3):

(z - h)(ρ - 3) = ρ(z - 1)

Expand:

zρ - 3z - hρ + 3h = ρz - ρ

Simplify:

zρ - 3z - hρ + 3h = ρz - ρ

Subtract zρ from both sides:

-3z - hρ + 3h = -ρ

Bring all terms to one side:

-3z - hρ + 3h + ρ = 0

Factor terms:

(-3z) + (-hρ + ρ) + 3h = 0

Factor ρ:

-3z + ρ( -h + 1 ) + 3h = 0 → equation (4)

Now, equation (4): -3z + ρ(1 - h) + 3h = 0

Let’s solve for z:

-3z = -ρ(1 - h) - 3h

Multiply both sides by (-1):

3z = ρ(1 - h) + 3h

Therefore,

z = [ρ(1<#sep#>_i), each term being of the form(1+ s_i), where the sum of the s_i is a.

Hence, $\frac{<#sep#>You are tasked with writing a unit test for the `convert_numbers_to_words` function using the `unittest` framework in Python. The function is defined in a module named `number_utils`. Your unit test should cover a variety of cases including positive and negative integers, positive and negative floating-point numbers, and zero. Your test should include at least five test cases.

**Sample Input and Output:**
- `convert_numbers_to_words(1)` should return `"one"`
- `convert_numbers_to_words(-12)` should return `"negative twelve"`
- `convert_numbers_to_words(0.25)` should return `"zero point two five"`
- `convert_numbers_to_words(-123.456)` should return `"negative one hundred twenty three point four five six"`
- `convert_numbers_to_words(0)` should return `"zero"`

```python
import unittest
from number_utils import convert_numbers_to_words

class TestNumberUtils(unittest.TestCase):
    """
    A test suite for the convert_numbers_to_words function.
    """

    def test_positive_integers(self):
        """
        Test conversion of positive integers to words.
        """
        self.assertEqual(convert_numbers_to_words(1), "one")
        self.assertEqual(convert_numbers_to_words(12), "twelve")
        self.assertEqual(convert_numbers_to_words(100), "one hundred")

    def test_negative_integers(self):
        """
        Test conversion of negative integers to words.
        """
        self.assertEqual(convert_numbers_to_words(-1), "negative one")
        self.assertEqual(convert_numbers_to_words(-12), "negative twelve")
        self.assertEqual(convert_numbers_to_words(-100), "negative one hundred")

    def test_positive_float(self):
        """
        Test conversion of positive floating-point numbers to words.
        """
        self.assertEqual(convert_numbers_to_words(1.5), "one point five")
        self.assertEqual(convert_numbers_to_words(0.25), "zero point two five")
        self.assertEqual(convert_numbers_to_words(123.456), "one hundred twenty three point four five six")

    def test_negative_float(self):
        """
        Test conversion of negative floating-point numbers to words.
        """
        self.assertEqual(convert_numbers_to_words(-1.5), "negative one point five")
        self.assertEqual(convert_numbers_to_words(-0.25), "negative zero point two five")
        self.assertEqual(convert_numbers_to_words(-123.456), "negative one hundred twenty three point four five six")

    def test_zero(self):
        """
        Test conversion of zero to words.
        """
        self.assertEqual(convert_numbers_to_words(0), "zero")

if __name__ == '__main__':
    unittest.main()
```<#sep#> terms:

(-3z) + (-hρ + ρ) + 3h = 0

Factor ρ:

-3z + ρ( -h + 1 ) + 3h = 0 → equation (4)

Now, equation (4): -3z + ρ(1 - h) + 3h = 0

Let’s solve for z:

-3z = -ρ(1 - h) - 3h

Multiply both sides by (-1):

3z = ρ(1 - h) + 3h

Therefore,

z = [ρ(1 - h) + 3h]/3 = [ρ(1 - h) + 3h]/3 → equation (5)

Now, substitute equation (5) into the constraint equation (3):

(ρ - 3)^2 + (z - 1)^2 = 1

Substituting z from equation (5):

(ρ - 3)^2 + ( [ρ(1 - h) + 3h]/3 - 1 )^2 = 1

Let’s simplify the second term:

[ρ(1 - h) + 3h]/3 - 1 = [ρ(1 - h) + 3h - 3]/3

Factor numerator:

= [ρ(1 - h) + 3(h - 1)]/3

Factor (h - 1):

= [ρ(1 - h) - 3(1 - h)]/3

Factor (1 - h):

= [(1 - h)(ρ - 3)] / 3

Therefore, the second term squared is [(1 - h)^2 (ρ - 3)^2]/9

So, substituting into equation (3):

(ρ - 3)^2 + [(1 - h)^2 (ρ - 3)^2]/9 = 1

Factor out (ρ - 3)^2:

(ρ - 3)^2 [1 + (1 - h)^2 / 9] = 1

Let’s write 1 as 9/9:

(ρ - 3)^2 [9/9 + (1 - h)^2 / 9] = 1

Combine terms:

(ρ - 3)^2 [9 + (1 - h)^2] / 9 = 1

Multiply both sides by 9:

(ρ - 3)^2 [9 + (1 - h)^2] = 9

Divide both sides by [9 + (1 - h)^2]:

(ρ - 3)^2 = 9 / [9 + (1 - h)^2]

Take square roots:

ρ - 3 = ± 3 / sqrt(9 + (1 - h)^2 )

Therefore,

ρ = 3 ± 3 / sqrt(9 + (1 - h)^2 )

Now, since ρ is the radial distance in cylindrical coordinates, and the torus is at ρ >= 3 - 1 = 2 and ρ <= 3 + 1 = 4. So ρ is between 2 and 4. The original sphere is at the center (0,0,h). So the minimal distance from (0,0,h) to the torus would be achieved at the point closest to the origin. Therefore, we take the negative sign in ρ = 3 - 3 / sqrt(...), because that would give a smaller ρ.

Therefore, take:

ρ = 3 - 3 / sqrt(9 + (1 - h)^2 )

Now, recall from equation (5):

z = [ρ(1 - h) + 3h]/3

Substitute ρ from above:

z = [ (3 - 3 / sqrt(9 + (1 - h)^2 )) (1 - h) + 3h ] / 3

Let me compute this step by step.

First, compute the term (3 - 3 / sqrt(...))(1 - h):

= 3(1 - h) - 3(1 - h)/sqrt(9 + (1 - h)^2 )

Then add 3h:

= 3(1 - h) - 3(1 - h)/sqrt(9 + (1 - h)^2 ) + 3h

Simplify:

= 3 - 3h - 3(1 - h)/sqrt(9 + (1 - h)^2 ) + 3h

The -3h and +3h cancel:

= 3 - 3(1 - h)/sqrt(9 + (1 - h)^2 )

Then divide by 3:

z = [3 - 3(1 - h)/sqrt(9 + (1 - h)^2 ) ] / 3

= 1 - (1 - h)/sqrt(9 + (1 - h)^2 )

So, z = 1 - (1 - h)/sqrt(9 + (1 - h)^2 )

Now, let's denote t = 1 - h. Then, h = 1 - t.

Substitute into z:

z = 1 - t / sqrt(9 + t^2 )

But z must also satisfy the equation from the parametrization of the torus. Wait, actually, we already used the parametrization to find z in terms of ρ and h, so this z is the z-coordinate of the point on the torus closest to (0,0,h).

But we need to relate this back to the original equation (3). Wait, no, because we already substituted the constraint. The problem is that we have expressions for ρ and z in terms of h, but we need to ensure that these satisfy the original equations.

Alternatively, since we already derived ρ and z in terms of h, maybe we can find h such that the minimal distance squared is equal to h^2. Wait, no. The sphere's radius is h, and the distance from (0,0,h) to the closest point on the torus must be equal to h.

But the distance squared from (0,0,h) to (ρ, z) is ρ^2 + (z - h)^2. So we need ρ^2 + (z - h)^2 = h^2.

But from equation (5), z is expressed in terms of ρ and h. So let's substitute that into ρ^2 + (z - h)^2 = h^2.

So, z from equation (5) is z = [ρ(1 - h) + 3h]/3

Therefore, (z - h) = [ρ(1 - h) + 3h]/3 - h = [ρ(1 - h) + 3h - 3h]/3 = [ρ(1 - h)]/3

So (z - h)^2 = [ρ^2 (1 - h)^2]/9

Therefore, the distance squared is ρ^2 + [ρ^2 (1 - h)^2]/9 = ρ^2 [1 + (1 - h)^2 /9]

But we have the constraint (ρ - 3)^2 + (z -1)^2 =1

From which we already derived ρ in terms of h.

But we also have ρ^2 + (z - h)^2 = h^2

Wait, but the distance squared from sphere center to the point on torus must equal h^2.

But we can use that equation.

So, substituting z - h from above:

ρ^2 + [ρ(1 - h)/3]^2 = h^2

So, ρ^2 [1 + (1 - h)^2 /9 ] = h^2

But from earlier, we have:

From equation (3):

(ρ - 3)^2 + (z - 1)^2 =1

But we also derived (ρ - 3)^2 = 9 / [9 + (1 - h)^2]

So, substituting into (ρ - 3)^2:

9 / [9 + (1 - h)^2 ] + (z - 1)^2 =1

But we also have from equation (5):

z = [ρ(1 - h) + 3h]/3

So, z -1 = [ρ(1 - h) + 3h -3]/3 = [ρ(1 - h) +3(h -1)]/3 = [ (1 - h)(ρ -3) ] /3

Therefore, (z -1)^2 = (1 - h)^2 (ρ -3)^2 /9

So substituting into the constraint equation:

(ρ -3)^2 + (1 - h)^2 (ρ -3)^2 /9 =1

Which simplifies to:

(ρ -3)^2 [1 + (1 - h)^2 /9 ]=1

Which is the same as before. Therefore, this seems redundant.

+
+    @staticmethod
+    def to_oauth2_message(content: dict, errors: dict) -> str:
+        """
+        Handle content as described in:
+            * https://tools.ietf.org/html/rfc6749#section-5.2
+            * https://tools.ietf.org/html/rfc6749#section-4.1.2.1
+            * https://tools.ietf.org/html/rfc6749#section-4.2.2.1
+        """
+
+        def _pop(key: str) -> str:
+            value = content.pop(key, None)
+            if value and isinstance(value, list):
+                value = value[0]
+            return value
+
+        if "error" in content:
+            error = _pop("error")
+            error_description = _pop("error_description") or errors.get(error)
+            message = f"{error}: {error_description}"
+            if "error_uri" in content:
+                message += f"\nMore information can be found on {_pop('error_uri')}"
+            if content:
+                message += f"\nAdditional information: {content}"
+        else:
+            message = f"{content}"
         return message

H<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `BookInventory` class in Python that manages a collection of books. Each book is uniquely identified by its title and author. The class should support the following operations:

1. **Add Book**: Add a book to the inventory. If the book already exists, update its quantity and price.
2. **Remove Book**: Remove a specified quantity of a book from the inventory. If the quantity to remove is greater than or equal to the current quantity, the book should be completely removed.
3. **Get Inventory Value**: Calculate and return the total value of the inventory, which is the sum of the product of quantity and price for each book.

Your class should have the following methods:
- `add_book(title, author, quantity, price)`: Adds a book to the inventory or updates it if it already exists.
- `remove_book(title, author, quantity)`: Removes a specified quantity of a book from the inventory.
- `get_inventory_value()`: Returns the total value of the inventory.

**Sample Input/Output**:
```python
inventory = BookInventory()
inventory.add_book("1984", "George Orwell", 5, 10.99)
inventory.add_book("To Kill a Mockingbird", "Harper Lee", 3, 12.99)
inventory.add_book("1984", "George Orwell", 2, 11.99)  # Update quantity and price
inventory.remove_book("1984", "George Orwell", 3)
print(inventory.get_inventory_value())  # Output: 41.97
```

```python
class BookInventory:
    def __init__(self):
        """Initialize the inventory dictionary."""
        self.inventory = {}

    def add_book(self, title, author, quantity, price):
        """
        Add a book to the inventory or update it if it already exists.
        
        :param title: The title of the book.
        :param author: The author of the book.
        :param quantity: The quantity of the book to add.
        :param price: The price of the book.
        """
        if (title, author) in self.inventory:
            self.inventory[(title, author)] = (self.inventory[(title, author)][0] + quantity, price)
        else:
            self.inventory[(title, author)] = (quantity, price)

    def remove_book(self, title, author, quantity):
        """
        Remove a specified quantity of a book from the inventory.
        
        :param title: The title of the book.
        :param author: The author of the book.
        :param quantity: The quantity of the book to remove.
        """
        if (title, author) in self.inventory:
            current_quantity, price = self.inventory[(title, author)]
            if quantity >= current_quantity:
                del self.inventory[(title, author)]
            else:
                self.inventory[(title, author)] = (current_quantity - quantity, price)

    def get_inventory_value(self):
        """
        Calculate and return the total value of the inventory.
        
        :return: The total value of the inventory.
        """
        total_value = 0
        for quantity, price in self.inventory.values():
            total_value += quantity * price
        return total_value
```
